And #76's blind spot (1) is fixed: the runner writes a `sentinel-heartbeat` **before** calling the route, so "the tick fired and the route died" no longer looks identical to "the tick never fired". That one also had a vacuous case — 18 tests passed first time and the status digit-strip survived deletion, because my "no response" fixture left the variable empty and the `:-0` default rendered it `0` either way. The real vector is that the status is `tail -1` of the response, so a transfer dying before curl's `-w` runs puts **body text** in the status variable.


## 2026-09-10 (late evening PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — a statistic was lying, autovacuum had never once run, and the second fix I tried had to be cancelled

**Trevor:** "Keep going and plan on working autonomously for the next 3 hours"

### Catching it live was the whole difference

I checked the instance mid-sweep and it was saturated — **34 active, 20 IO waiters**. Instead of reading job history afterwards I took two `pg_statio_user_tables` snapshots 225 seconds apart, and the top reader was a table I had never looked at: **`net._http_response`, 589 blocks/s, 2.8× the next one** — while sitting only **4th** by cumulative reads, which is exactly why every previous sweep missed it.

⭐ The live wait breakdown also answered a question the scheduler tables cannot: **12 sessions on `IO/DataFileRead`, 9 on `IPC/BufferIo`, zero on any lock.** It is IO. And `authenticator` held 9 of 12 — REST-side, which corroborates yesterday's wallet-backfill conclusion on a third instrument.

### The mechanism was a statistic, not a query

pg_net's TTL pruning works fine. But `n_dead_tup` read **0** on a table that actually held **161,390** dead tuples, and `autovacuum_count` was **0 for the table's entire life**. Autovacuum's threshold is computed *from* that number, so a stat pinned at zero makes the trigger unreachable **by construction** — the deletes happen forever and the vacuum never does. 8.4 GB of TOAST holding 443 MB of live data.

✅ One `ANALYZE`, **581 ms**, and autovacuum fired **43 seconds later** and cleared all 161,390. The satisfying part is that the fix doesn't substitute for the estate's maintenance — it unblocks it.

### Then I tried the second lever and had to cancel it

A manual `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` is **unthrottled**. It blew past the 60-second timeout, was still in `DataFileRead` at 66 s, and pushed `io_waiters` from 0–2 to **6** — on an instance with a documented saturation problem, in a wallet-backfill hour. I cancelled it and then **checked rather than assumed**: no vacuum running, **zero cron failures** in the window.

⭐ **That failure is worth more than the fix.** The obvious remediation here is a scheduled maintenance vacuum, and there is even a precedent job to copy. **It would reproduce exactly the IO spike I just had to kill.** Autovacuum is throttled; a manual vacuum is not. Measured: 581 ms versus over 60 seconds.

### What I did not settle

Whether the stat keeps tracking on its own. It read 238 after my ANALYZE — **but ANALYZE sets that value directly, so that number may be my own measurement rather than the collector recovering.** The test is a re-read with no manual ANALYZE, and it decides whether a scheduled job is needed at all.

And the 8.4 GB itself stays. Plain vacuum only marks space reusable; reclaiming it needs `VACUUM FULL` and an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock across every pg_net lane. That is a maintenance window and Trevor's call, not something to do autonomously at midnight.

## 2026-09-10 (morning PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — the new arm caught the recurrence on day one, and the data killed the explanation we had already accepted

**Trevor:** "Keep going"

### The arm earned itself immediately

`check_pgcron_failure_rate('6 hours')` read **158 fails across 38 jobs** on its first real day. Yesterday nothing watched this surface at all; today it is the thing that said "look here."

### The bursts recur on a clock

`job startup timeout` lands at **09-09 12Z, 13Z, 18Z** and again at **09-10 12Z, 13Z** — and at **zero of the other 43 hours** in the window. #73 is written as a *nine-hour spell*. It is a **daily pattern at fixed hours**, and that reframing matters more than any number in it.

### Then the part I did not expect

#73 records the amplifier as the six-hourly heavies clustering at 0/6/12/18 and overlapping. ⛔ **The scheduled population is identical across all four windows, and at 0Z and 6Z the identical cluster finishes in seconds.** Same job, by hour: 51 s · 27 s · **780 s** · **530 s**. Another: 9 s · 7 s · **600 s** · **688 s**.

⭐ **The variable is the hour, not the schedule.** So the reflex fix — stagger the `*/6` jobs — cannot work, and I would have reached for it. The explanation was already on the page, already believed, and 48 hours of hourly data disposed of it.

### And `job startup timeout` turned out to be the symptom

At **09-09 12:36Z, 29 runs across 21 distinct jobs ended inside one minute, with durations spanning 0 to 788 seconds.** ⭐ **A shared duration is a timeout; a shared instant across heterogeneous durations is a global unblock** — that one distinction is what turned a pile of failures into a mechanism.

Several of those jobs returned `1 row` after 300–780 seconds. They were **waiting, not computing**. The startup timeouts fire eleven seconds after a launch attempt because the six slots are still held by the stalled jobs; then everything releases at once and a backlog flushes at 0.1 s a job.

I also ruled out the obvious lock explanation for the price of one query — all 11 bare-refresh jobs already use `CONCURRENTLY` — and wrote that down so nobody re-runs the hunt.

### What I did not do, and why

`pipeline_runs` says hourly load varies **3.1×** while pg_cron's population is flat, so the extra load belongs to the other schedulers. That is a real narrowing. ⛔ But my interaction hypothesis has a live counterexample in it — 0Z carries *more* runs than 12Z and stays clean — so I stated the counterexample rather than omitting it, and changed nothing. **The levers here are capacity and other people's schedules, and neither should move on a hypothesis I can already falsify myself.**


### Postscript, an hour later: I resolved my own counterexample, and the fix was a better denominator

I ended the entry above with an honest gap — 0Z carries *more* `pipeline_runs` than 12Z and stays clean, so run count could not separate them. **The count was the wrong denominator.**

Split by lane and it falls apart immediately: the load is the **`wallet-backfill` family**, and it runs **1,728 times at 13Z, 1,636 at 18Z, 1,456 at 07Z — and exactly zero at fourteen of the twenty-four hours.**

✅ **Both single-factor controls were sitting in the data.** 06Z: the six-hourly cluster with **zero** wallet runs → clean. 07Z: 1,456 wallet runs with **no** cluster → clean. 13Z and 18Z: both → bursts. That is an interaction with each factor independently exonerated, which is a much stronger thing than the correlation I had an hour ago.

⭐ And the overlap explains itself from a schedule already written down in #48: the 0Z and 6Z wallet cohorts land their work in the *following* hour and miss the heavies; the 12Z heavies run 600–869 s and are still holding slots when 13Z's flood arrives; at 18Z the cohort and the heavies overlap head-on.

⛔ I changed nothing, again. A plausible mechanism is not a measurement. But it gives #48's untested experiment a second and independent reason to run, a cheaper probe than the one it proposes, and a falsifier I wrote down before anyone runs it.

⚠ And the caveat stays in view rather than being quietly dropped: burst-hour run count may be partly an *effect*, since wallets re-dispatch ~9× and die more often when the instance is busy. **The 06Z/07Z pair is what carries the argument, because there the 0-versus-1,456 split is schedule, not retry.**
## 2026-09-09 (late evening PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — I went to widen a guard's scope, and measurement said the scope was right and my fix would have made it worse

**Trevor:** "Keep going"

### The gap was real and the fix was wrong

`check_cron_heavy_job_exec_drift` filters `WHERE j.username = 'cron_heavy'`. It inspects **58** job/function pairs while **55 active jobs run as `postgres`** — half the scheduled population, invisible by construction. That is textbook *"ask what a passing guard is structurally silent about"*, and I went to widen the filter.

⛔ **Then I checked whether the widened assertion could ever fail, and it could not.** `postgres` **owns** all 54 public functions its own cron jobs call, and an owner's EXECUTE cannot be revoked. Widening the `WHERE` would have added **55 assertions that cannot fail** — coverage on paper, nothing underneath. ⭐ **A guard I'd have shipped feeling good about, that would have made the next reader trust a number that meant less than the one it replaced.**

ⓘ Worth separating: it is not the superuser rule. `postgres` has `rolsuper = false` here — only `supabase_admin` is super. **Ownership** is what makes those pairs immune, and I only know that because I checked instead of reasoning from the role's name.

### So what was actually broken

The filter was **correct** — for a reason nobody wrote down and nothing enforced. ⭐ **The guard's blast radius was pinned to a role NAME rather than to the PROPERTY that makes a pair testable.** The day a job is scheduled under a role that doesn't own its target function, the name filter silently fails to cover it and nobody finds out — which is the same failure the guard itself exists to catch, one level up.

It now walks every active job and splits by that property: `inspected` **113** · `immune_owner` **55** (tested by nothing, and it says so) · `revocable` **58** (what it actually tests) · `offenders` **0**. **Reading `inspected` as coverage is now impossible.**

### The controls, because a zero without one is worthless

Across the 51 functions `cron_heavy` is scheduled to call, `has_function_privilege` reads **true for `cron_heavy` 51/51** and **false for `anon` 0/51** — so the predicate is not a constant. And `cron_heavy` owns **0** of the 51, so its EXECUTE is grant-derived and genuinely revocable. Nothing was mutated to establish either.

The smoke arm now floors `revocable` rather than `inspected`, and hard-fails a run that inspected 113 while testing 2 as **vacuous, not clean**. ⚠ Mutation-proven both ways — and the second mutation is the one worth remembering: replacing the three-state read with `?? 0` reds **14** tests. **The shape this codebase is organised around, caught in the arm I was writing to catch it.**

### Two things re-derived and deliberately not acted on

**#43's population has not drained** — 50 inert `statement_timeout` declarations today against the filing's 48, the working form still used by exactly 7 jobs, unchanged in 14 days. ⚠ But my role split (27/23) does not reproduce the filing's (36/12), so **I did not claim the split moved**: a method that cannot reproduce the recorded value cannot measure a change in it. And #43's own worked example, jobid 256, no longer exists.

**`rpc-ts-listings-atlas-sync`** — the saturation spell's top offender, 55 failures in the 6 h window — is **not** chronically broken: 09-07 **0.0%** · 09-08 **0.4%** · 09-09 **26.5%** (191) · 09-10 **3.3%**. The failures were the spell. Checking cost one query and stopped a hunt.


### Postscript: the same question, a second guard, and the answer was "don't"

`sales-batch-insert-23505-guard` is scoped to one directory and excludes the backfill routes on a **claim about their code**. Nothing enforced that claim, so I checked it: all 30 files referencing `23505`, every handler outside the scope read by hand. **Population zero, exclusion still true.**

⛔ **Then I did not widen it, and the reason is the useful part.** The property is *batch insert + 23505 swallowed with no row-by-row fallthrough*, and **the fallthrough half is not reliably visible to a source scan.** My prototype produced twelve candidates and all twelve were false positives — mostly the row-by-row retry itself, whose `.insert(row)` argument is an identifier and therefore looks batch-shaped. ⭐ **A guard with that false-positive rate is worse than none: it teaches readers to skim.** Same instinct as the cron guard, opposite conclusion, and both times it was measurement that decided.

### And a small piece of #70

The upstream probe is **confirmed impossible from here** rather than assumed — the proxy answers `403 CONNECT`, `connect_rejected — policy denial`. From our own tables the second All Day lane moves in lockstep with the sales lane, which **disfavours** a defect specific to the sales query. ⚠ It is **not** an independent control and I said so: both routes read the same Flow REST host.

🚨 The part I'd have got wrong by reading the obvious number: raw weekly totals say *the slide is accelerating*. The current week is **partial** — 234 runs against 551–725 — and **per run it recovered on both lanes.** A delta between two stocks is neither a rate nor a sign, and this one would have shipped as a headline.
## 2026-09-09 (evening PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — I went to unblock someone else's filing and ended up refuting the two facts it rested on

**Trevor:** "Keep going and looking for additional tasks to complete for the next 3 hours autonomously"

### The question was narrow and the answer was not

The 09-08 filing left exactly one blocker: *name the live path keeping `pack_distributions` fresh, then jobid 15 can be paused.* Its author had done the honest thing — enumerated six caller sources, found nothing, and **declined to guess.**

⭐ **The path exists and the enumeration cannot reach it: a Supabase Edge Function invoked by an external scheduler.** `pg_stat_statements` gave it away in one query — the RPC arrives wrapped in `pgrst_source`, which means it came over HTTP, so pg_cron, triggers and every in-database caller are excluded at a stroke. ⚠ **That wrapper is a free discriminator and I want it in the standing rule: check it before concluding "no caller."** The timestamps then placed the schedule (minute :13, four hours apart) and a GitHub Actions comment written for an unrelated reason corroborated it.

### And then the safety argument dissolved

That filing's case for pausing jobid 15 was *"the supply data is provably fresh from elsewhere"*, resting on two observations. ⛔ **Both are artifacts.** `total_sealed` is a GENERATED column — `GREATEST(total_minted - total_opened, 0)` — so "populated on 2,099 of 2,099" is a tautology, not a reading. And `pack_distributions.updated_at` is bumped every four hours by the catalog lane, which **says in its own source that it deliberately does not write the supply columns.**

🚨 The supply behind it: **median as-of 2026-06-28**, 2,068 of 2,085 rows older than 30 days, newest successful fetch two days before the upstream died. `pack_distributions` mirrors it on **2,083 of 2,083 rows, zero divergence in either direction** — with a positive control, because a zero is worthless without one: the same comparison **does** report a difference on the two rows whose fetch failed.

### The part worth carrying

⭐ **Four layers, each individually defensible, whose net effect is that a 73-day-old number is indistinguishable from a fresh one.** A lane dies. Its failure is recorded honestly — `topshot_pack_supply` has `supply_ok`, `supply_err`, and stores NULL rather than zero, exactly the shape this repo keeps asking for. **Nothing reads those columns; a grep across `app`, `components` and `lib` returns zero matches.** A sibling lane keeps bumping the row's `updated_at`. A generated column keeps returning a value. And the one alert named for this table keys on *"has a new distribution appeared"* while labelled `type: data_stale, pipeline: pack_distributions` — silent about the four columns every pack surface renders, and it would not have fired at any point in the drift.

⚠ **I did not claim a user is seeing a wrong number, and the reasons are in the filing rather than implied.** The highest-stakes surface is already drained to zero behind an honest branch, the pack page is careful about NULL-vs-0, and pack supply is genuinely slow-moving. **What is established is that nothing can tell the difference.**

### Why I shipped nothing

⛔ Pausing jobid 15 costs less than it saves in the wrong direction: 1 run/day, the only lane that resumes automatically if the upstream returns, and the last daily record that it is still dead. ⛔ A supply-age alert arm would fire immediately and stay red indefinitely — this estate's own permanently-red-instrument trap, and the fifth layer of noise rather than the first of signal. **The useful version needs a decision that is Trevor's.** So: a filing, a register entry, and a stated recommendation that the cheapest real win is not an alert at all — render the as-of date next to the number.

## 2026-09-09 (evening PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — the pg_cron failure surface was watched by a function nobody calls, and I corrected my own "recovered" call

**Trevor:** "Keep going and looking for additional tasks to complete for the next 3 hours autonomously"

### The gap, and why nobody had seen it

**Every sentinel arm reads `pipeline_runs`. A pg_cron job that fails to START never writes one** — `log_pipeline_run` sits inside the body, which never runs. I verified it by grep rather than by reasoning: `app/api/sentinel/route.ts` had **zero references to `cron.job_run_details`**.

⭐ And the one instrument that does read it — `check_pgcron_recent_failures()` — **has no caller anywhere in the repo.** It appears only inside *other migrations' comments*, thoughtfully discussing what it can and cannot catch. So on 09-09 the estate logged **399 cron failures against a 0–3-per-day baseline** and five sentinel sweeps saw none of them. **A function nobody calls is not an instrument.**

### Where I was wrong, twice, and how I found out

⚠ **My first framing of that function was "it sees nothing." Wrong.** Its `where l.status = 'failed'` filter means it reports the *currently* broken. Replayed at four probe times: the worst job **would** have surfaced (fails 47 → 174); two others with 23 and 17–19 failures were invisible at every probe because their latest tick happened to succeed. The real gap is narrower and more specific than the one I set out to describe — and only a replay showed it.

🚨 **Then two defects in my own arm, both caught by tests rather than by reading it.** The first is the exact error this whole codebase is organised around: `Number(r.runs ?? 0) || 0` **collapses "the RPC returned no payload" into "the RPC answered zero runs"** — a failed read published as a measurement, **inside an arm whose entire subject is measurement honesty.** Seven existing sentinel tests went red; that is how I learned. Fixing it left the later field reads unguarded, so a null payload threw a raw `TypeError` into the catch and the report said *"Exception: Cannot read properties of null"* — a driver message leaked into a health report, with the honest branch never reached.

⭐ **Both times the tests knew before I did.** I had read that code three times.

### And I corrected my own conclusion from a few hours earlier

I reported the spell **recovered**. The spell had. But **statement-timeout cancellations never reached zero** — 2, then 0, then 11, then 1 across successive hours, against a baseline of 0–3 per *day*. ⭐ **My call rested on the indicators I happened to check — startup timeouts, IO waiters, median duration — and not on the one still firing.** The new arm's own "negative control" window is what surfaced it, on its first use.

⚠ **Reattributed rather than re-filed:** every one dies at ~120 s, not `cron_heavy`'s 600 s — `username = postgres`, no `SET statement_timeout` prefix, and that role carries none in `rolconfig`. It is the 120 s-clipping class filed on 08-31, not spell residue.

### The finding that changes a nine-day-old recommendation

That filing is excellent and its cases are drained. But **my five jobs are different from its five**, because its method finds jobs clipped **at rest** and **load recruits others** from the 36 prefix-less jobs. So the class cannot be closed by draining a list.

⛔ **And there is now a second reason not to apply its fix, independent of the one it gives.** Its argument was **waste** (a 120 s kill becomes a 600 s kill). Mine is **contention**: with `max_worker_processes = 6`, raising these jobs 120 s → 600 s multiplies worker-slot occupancy ~5× **on the exact constraint that produced this incident's 135 startup timeouts.** Applying the prefix would have made today worse. It is a trade-off across two budgets now, not a one-liner — so nothing was applied, and the filing says why.

### Three more of my own defects, and the one that was a defect in a FIX

🚨 **The arm cost 18,313 physical buffer reads (~143 MB) per call** — an arm about IO saturation that was itself a 143 MB read every sweep. I found it with a pre-flight `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)`, not by it hurting. `cron.job_run_details` has no index on `start_time`, so a 6 h filter seq-scans 249,731 rows to find 2,174. ⛔ **The obvious fix was unavailable and I tested that rather than assuming it:** the table is `supabase_admin`-owned and `postgres` is not a member. Fenced the scan on the runid PK instead — **628 reads, identical answers.**

⭐ **Then the fence needed its own honesty branch, and that branch needed to be reachable.** A fence that cut the window short would publish an under-count as a measurement — the same failed-read-as-fact shape, one level down — so the payload carries `complete`. 🚨 **But my control for it would not bind: the fence scales with the window, so no window binds it, and no synthetic row can be inserted to force one. `complete` was unfalsifiable in production — indistinguishable from a flag hard-wired true, feeding a dead branch.** That is this file's own "prove a watcher can see a FAILURE" rule, and I had shipped the opposite. Fixed with a test-only fence-span override and proven live in both directions, with a no-change control the fix cannot move: span 100 → false/100, span 50000 → true/2174, default → true/2174, identical to before.

🚨 **And I retracted my own baseline.** I sampled 7 days, called it "the baseline", and it was the 7 days *after* a change point. Re-measured over 30: **08-11..08-30 at 50–496 failures/DAY, 08-31..09-08 at 0–3/day, 09-09 at 399.** ⚠ The inverse of the pooled-rate rule already in CLAUDE.md — a rate pooled *across* a fix measures the fix's absence; a rate sampled only *after* one mistakes the fix for the norm. ⭐ The 08-31 top-consumer IO drain is the plausible cause (sharp change point, sensible mechanism) but several things shipped that day, so it is recorded as correlation. **Nobody claimed that benefit at the time: the drain's own entry talks only about IO, so ~200 cron failures/day were removed by a change measured on a different axis.**

### What shipped

`check_pgcron_failure_rate(p_window)` and a `pg_cron Failures (6h)` arm. ⭐ Its detail splits startup-timeout from statement-timeout counts deliberately: **that split is the discriminator between worker-slot contention and a clipped tail — same failure count, opposite causes, opposite fixes** — and it is the thing that took longest to find by hand. It also covers the 11 of 134 cron jobs that are bare `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW` and write no pipeline row at all.

ⓘ It does **not** supersede `Pipeline Silence`, which answered correctly that day. Saying so matters: an arm that oversells its own coverage is how the next reader stops checking the other one.

## 2026-09-09 (midday PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — I used a control to exonerate my own change, then didn't ask what the control meant. It was a live incident.

**Trevor:** "Dig deeper"

### The one sentence worth keeping

**A signal strong enough to exonerate a change is strong enough to be a finding.** Three hours earlier I proved a deploy of mine innocent with a no-change control — *the whole fleet got slower, so my probe moved with its siblings, not against them.* That reasoning was right. And then the control sat unexamined, **because it had already done its job.** Everything below came from finally turning around and asking what it was saying.

### What it was saying

The instance stepped into a saturation spell at **10:00Z** and was still in it nine hours later, worsening: fleet median pipeline duration 2,988 ms at 08Z → **30,921 ms at 18Z**, against a **flat 09-07 and 09-08 at the same hours**. That last comparison is the whole reason this is a finding rather than "the known daytime band" — two prior days refute it.

⭐ **The part I would not have predicted: `max_worker_processes = 6`.** Connections were fine (45 of 90); the binding resource is worker slots. pg_cron runs every job as a background worker, and the heavy six-hourly MV refreshes are clustered inside a 30-minute window of hours 0/6/12/18. Yesterday they took 12–93 s and never overlapped. Today they take 250–600 s and **collide** — I caught three running at once. The result is **270+ `job startup timeout` failures**, bursting at exactly hours 12, 13 and 18.

⛔ **And a job that fails to START writes no `pipeline_runs` row**, so every pipeline monitor sees silence rather than failure. This is the same shape CLAUDE.md recorded *yesterday* for the REVOKE/grant case, arrived at from a completely different direction.

### Three hypotheses I killed, which is most of the value

- **Autovacuum.** A 48-minute `VACUUM ANALYZE wallet_moments_cache` was at the head of the activity list — a beautiful culprit. It finished, and the instance stayed at 12 active / 11 IO waiters. Symptom, not driver.
- **An invalid index.** Zero in `public`.
- **An FMV propagation wave.** Writes did step up at 10Z — but **yesterday hit a higher rate with only a mild slowdown**, so volume alone is not sufficient.

⭐ Root cause is **not settled**, and I wrote that down instead of picking the most narratable candidate. `wallet_moments_cache` is the common denominator and is explicitly labelled UNPROVEN — including the caveat that its `autovacuum_count` of 963 has **no window** (`stats_reset` is NULL), so it is not a rate and I must not quote it as one.

### The instrument finding, where nothing was broken and nothing lied

The sentinel saw it. It ran mid-incident, named the right lane twice, and reported **six of sixteen checks INCONCLUSIVE — including `Trust Health`, the master arm.** Overall status: **WARN**. GHA: green. `ops_alert_dedup`: empty for fourteen hours.

The per-check rule that produces that — *a statement timeout is not data loss, so warn, don't page* — is **correct and was earned by two documented false pages**. I nearly filed it as the defect. It isn't.

⭐⭐ **The gap is one level up: overall status is `checks.some(status === "warn")` — a `some()`, not a count.** One warn and thirteen warns are identical. **Every check reasons correctly in isolation, and no check can see the population.** "My query timed out" is a weak statement; "six of us timed out at once" is a strong one, and nothing in the system could make it.

So: a `Measurement Blackout` arm that **issues no query** — the one check that sharpens as the database degrades — capped structurally at `warn`, and `ok`-but-visible below threshold per the route's own anti-chronic-warn convention.

### The part I got wrong, caught before shipping

I keyed the arm on the `INCONCLUSIVE` label and replayed the **real** 18:47Z payload through it rather than a fixture I'd written. It counted **four**, below its own threshold. **It would have stayed silent on the exact incident that motivated it.** Two checks time out with the identical error and are never labelled, because they build their detail by hand.

⭐ The fix is not to go label those two — it's to stop trusting the label. The arm keys on the **condition** now, and the real payload is a verbatim fixture, so label-only detection fails with *"expected 4 to be 6"*. **A fixture I write tests what I believe; the production payload tests what is true.**

### And what I didn't do

⛔ Nothing operational. Capacity is Trevor's call, and every lever I had — unschedule a heavy job, stagger the `*/6` block — treats a symptom on an unproven mechanism. CLAUDE.md already records a stagger that was REFUTED. Shipping the instrument is safe; shipping a guess at 13 active / 13 IO waiters is how last night's jobid-481 mistake happens in reverse.

## 2026-09-09 (morning PT) · Claude Code (cloud) — a top-3 database trap had no tree-wide instrument, and the guard that "covered" it was a list of six files

**Trevor:** "Work on anything you can find on our to do lists, Claude.md, roadmaps, pipelines, sentinel issues, known issues, or anything else… Also look for CI improvements."

### The pass in one line

I went looking for CI improvements and found that the most-repeated database trap in CLAUDE.md — PostgREST silently clamping a `.limit()` above 1,000 — was guarded by a **curated list of six already-fixed routes**. A tree walk found **21 sites carrying the shape, none of them on that list**, and two of them truncating today.

### Why the existing guard could not see them

`__tests__/invariants-postgrest-cap.test.ts` is a good regression pin: it names the six routes that were fixed after the class bit production five times, and asserts each still reads `fmv_current` or pages with `.range()`. It cannot fail for a seventh site, because it never looks for one. ⭐ **CLAUDE.md already says this in as many words — *prefer a tree walk over a curated list and a ban at zero over an allowlist; make suppression the curated list* — and the rule simply had not been applied to the class its own DB section names.**

⭐ **The tell here is a NUMBER, not a copy.** This repo's usual instruction is *"grep for the EXPRESSION, not the file"*, and it does not work on this class: every instance spells the number differently (2000, 3000, 5000, 8000, 10000, 20000, 100000, 200000). That is exactly what a tree walk with a numeric predicate is for.

### What `.limit(20000)` actually is

Not a bound. PostgREST returns 1,000 rows and says nothing, so the number is an **unstated claim that the population is smaller than it** — written in the one syntax that reads as an enforced limit. Anything computed underneath (a total, a map, `rows.length`) is then built on a truncation no caller can see.

### The measurement point: why I did NOT just rewrite every number to 1,000

It looks free. It is not. **Lowering an over-population bound to `.limit(1000)` is behaviour-preserving only if the server cap really is 1,000** — and that value is a PostgREST setting, not readable from Postgres. ⭐ **I tested the egress rather than assuming it: the agent proxy rejects CONNECT to the REST host, so I could not measure the cap from here.** Cutting a bound over a population of 838,392 on an unverified premise would have introduced the very truncation I was there to remove.

So the rule became: **measure the population first.** Sixteen sites measured under the cap were lowered to a bound that is TRUE and now carry the measured count and the date. The four at or over it keep their numbers and carry a marker stating the measurement instead.

### The two that truncate today

- **`pinnacle-metadata-backfill`'s Q3/Q4 discovery pools** — `.limit(5000)`/`.limit(8000)` over ≥9,000 rows, **with no `.order()`**, so which 1,000 it sees is undefined and the candidate filter runs client-side over the physical head. Classic queue-walk-from-the-top. ⚠ **But I measured the blast radius instead of leading with the scary number: only 419 distinct composite `edition_key`s exist across the pool and Q4's target set was 12 rows.** "Some keys may be unreachable" is the honest claim; "the tail is never repaired" would have been the exciting one.
- **`compute-topshot-pack-ev`'s ask-anchor fallback** — 1,995 rows read at `.limit(3000)`, so about half, unordered. ⭐ **`lib/packs/pack-deals.ts` hit the same wall on the same table and fixed it by calling `get_pack_ask_state_map()` — same defect, same table, sibling file, fixed in one and not the other.** ⛔ Left alone because the function is **dormant**, verified rather than assumed: no `cron.job` row, no `pipeline_runs` row in 24h, and `refresh_atlas_pack_ev()` (jobid 217) does the work.

### The endpoint I withdrew, and the test that was holding the defect in place

`/api/pack-listings/historical-pulls` read 838,392 rows at `.limit(20000)` (→1,000, unordered) and published the result as a pack's `total` pulls; a failed read returned `{ total: 0 }`. ⭐ **And a green test asserted that**, titled `returns total 0 when the acquisitions query errors` — a passing test stating the promise is what holds it in place, so it is **inverted, never deleted**.

⛔ **The reason it cannot simply be repaired is the third defect, and I only found it by reading the data rather than the code:** `moment_acquisitions.pack_dist_id` — the column that would tie a pull to a pack — is **NULL on all 838,392 rows**. That is why the route reached for a loose token match on `set_name` in the first place. **Caller named before touching it: zero.** Withdrawn to a 501 rather than deleted, so the diagnosis stays where the next reader meets it.

### Two method notes

- ⭐ **I extracted the suppression-window helper instead of copying it.** The divisor ratchet had bought three properties painfully (a same-line window is unreachable because these expressions wrap; a fixed 3-line lookback silently ignored two real suppressions on 09-07; the block walk must stop at code). A second copy would have re-earned all three. `scripts/lib/marker-suppression.mjs` now holds one implementation, and the divisor guard's **own** four controls still drive it — 9/9 after the migration, so the extraction is proved by tests that already existed.
- ⭐ **Mutation-checked in both directions.** A synthetic offender injected into `lib/seo.ts` reds the guard at the right line; neutering all four real markers reds it with exactly those four. "It passes" was never the evidence.

### And what I did NOT change

ⓘ **CLAUDE.md is untouched on purpose.** Every rule this pass needed was already in it. **Nothing new was learned — a documented trap simply had no instrument** — and adding a line would have spent scarce headroom (39,895/40,000) restating text that was already there and unapplied.

ⓘ **Two things checked and left, because a sweep that cannot return "not a defect" is a closing spree:** `sniper-feed`'s `fetchRetiredMomentIds` fails OPEN on a paged read, but `moments.retired` has 0 true rows (re-verified today) so it is latent and its own comment says so; and `lib/market-sources`' `return out`-on-error partial map serves `/api/market-truth`, which has **zero callers** and is already recorded as dead in that file's header.
