  the parking commit carried only the README pointing at it. Re-derived from the ledger spec, shipped
  with two mutation checks. (The concurrent session then fixed `.gitignore` at the mechanism.)

⭐ **Method notes.** A `git clone --depth 1` of the public repo works from the cloud container and is
a far better test tree than the tar recipe; changes still cross to the push clone as `git diff`
patches applied with `git apply`, never whole files. The device push clone needs
`GIT_AUTHOR_NAME='Claude (Cowork)'` per call, and the credential file is a full URL —
`"${U}/owner/repo.git"`, never spliced. Mobile layout is still unmeasurable from here: Chrome's
`resize_window` reports success and `innerWidth` stays 1536.

Left alone, stated: the `saved-wallets` quota check fails OPEN on a DB error (a documented decision;
cap = 1 free wallet, monetization tabled); ~200 `const { data } = await …` sites without `error`
remain under the existing ratchet; `edge-fn-drift` red 12× is by design (0 safe to redeploy).
## 2026-09-03 (daytime PT) · two rate limiters that could not fire, and an ETA computed from a rate that had stopped

Continuation of the overnight run. The through-line this time is **guards and alerts that were
structurally incapable of doing their job while every instrument read healthy** — and, twice, the
discovery that *my own* first diagnosis of the fix was wrong.

**The MCP daily cap could not fire, for two independent reasons.** `check_feature_quota` gates every
authed request to the MCP worker and the README advertised a `429`. (a) The quota rows were keyed to
plans **`pro`** and **`partner`**, which `pro_users_plan_check` FORBIDS — so the two rows carrying the
real caps were unreachable **by construction, provable from the schema rather than argued**, and all 20
active `pro_grandfather` wallets took the `no_quota_configured_failing_open` branch. (b) The counted key
`'mcp_query'` was **written by nothing** — `mcp_log_tool_call` writes `'mcp_' || tool_name` — so
`used_today` was pinned at 0 for every plan, `free` included. ⭐ **My first diagnosis of (b) said it
needed a `wrangler deploy` no session can run. It did not** — the Worker already CALLS that function, so
the missing write belonged in the function it was already calling. *Check what the caller already calls
before concluding a fix is out of reach.* ⚠ Both halves were **unexercised** (zero `mcp%` rows all-time),
and the first attempt to measure that used the WRONG KEY.

**The backlog alert published a three-week ETA off a rate that had stopped.** `days_to_drain` divides
the pile by `outflow_24h`, a **trailing** count. 🚨 **The published number went UP (25.1 → 32.6 days)
while the true rate went DOWN**, because the numerator barely moves and the stale burst ages out slowly
— so on a decaying series it reads plausible at every refresh **and errs toward reassurance**. Against
the same table and column over a shorter window: `outflow_3h` = **10** against 1,263/24h where steady
state gives ~158, i.e. an honest ETA nearer **526 days**. The resolver was HEALTHY and out of tractable
work (candidates/hour 1,031 → 17, nearly all `onchain_nil`) — **not a broken pipeline, an alert making a
claim its data cannot support.**

⭐ **The half that nearly got missed, and the most transferable thing here.** Nulling the ETA made the
alert honest and it *looked done* — the existing `COALESCE` dropped the sentence cleanly. What remained
was *"Live inflow 1/24h vs outflow 1263/24h"*: every number TRUE, and read alone it says the pile is
draining briskly. **The reader reaches the same false conclusion from facts instead of from a claim.**
So "we stopped saying the wrong thing" is not the exit condition; **"a reader cannot conclude the wrong
thing" is.** Three states — draining · stalled · never-flowed — and the middle one has to be SAID.
Promoted to the honesty canon as the inverse of the empty-state rule: **silence is not neutrality when
the surrounding context is directional.**

**Two reds on `main` were mine, and both are worth naming.** A duplicate register ID (`R84`) that two
sessions took independently — git merged both cleanly because the rows are different lines in different
sections, so **a rebase reporting no conflict is not evidence there is no collision**, and the red
surfaced on the OTHER session's commit. And a migration whose `REVOKE` named only `public` — the rule is
in CLAUDE.md, enforced by a guard I never looked for because I had grepped for guards reading the ledger
and the register but not `supabase/migrations/`. **Not a live exposure** (`CREATE OR REPLACE` does not
reset an ACL; anon read false before and after), and the `anon-exec: intentional` marker was deliberately
NOT used — it would have satisfied the guard with a false claim.

**A near-clobber avoided by diffing rather than choosing.** A stash-pop offered a version of `R78`
**667 characters shorter** than upstream's, on a row I had not touched. ⚠ The comparison itself nearly
misled me: `awk length()` counts BYTES, python `len()` counts CODE POINTS — the same line measured 3,672
and 3,620.

**Verification discipline that paid.** Every guard was made to FAIL before being trusted (dropping the
stall guard, `<`→`<=`, widening the window; un-suppressing `mcp_query`, emptying the walk). The backlog
watch was closed by the **real caller** — the pg_cron tick at 18:29:00.064Z, not a manual run. And
`check_cursor_stall_threshold_drift()` returning **one row** was nearly reported as a finding: it is the
jsonb-array shape holding `[]`, i.e. clean. **Read the return TYPE before interpreting the count.**

Shipped: register **R83** and **R85** both closed; four migrations; two new CI guards; a re-pin
(pin + migration + guard registration). Left open on purpose: whether a stalled 42k backlog deserves
severity above `info` — a product call, Trevor's.


## 2026-09-03 (early hours PT) · five ships, and three of them came out of verifying the previous one

An autonomous overnight run. The through-line is that **each fix was found by checking the one before
it**, not by a fresh sweep — which is the cheapest source of real defects this session has found.

**The pack-detail header was quoting a fourteen-day-old severity as if it were today's.** 124/86/26
users became **6/13/1** on the same grouping — a 7–20× drop, and the remainder is the honest-degradation
path *firing* rather than the hang it replaced. ⛔ And the obvious follow-up is measured dead:
`get_pack_lifecycle` runs **5–32 ms** warm over a deterministic hash sample against a **5,000 ms**
budget, so an overrun is instance contention (R46), not the query. An afternoon avoided.

**The intermittently-red `edge-deno` job was wall-clock dependent, and the fix overturns a filed
decision.** supabase-js 2.113.0 aged past Deno's 24 h minimum-dependency-age at 06:04:34Z; its own
auth-js at **06:05:01Z**. For those **27 seconds no resolvable graph existed**, and the run inside them
was a hard error. ⚠ **Tuning the threshold cannot fix it** — any non-zero age has a boundary and the
sub-package skew straddles it wherever it sits. `.gitignore` said a lockfile was *"deliberately NOT
committed … introduces a lock-mismatch failure mode"* — **a cost stated with no number in it**, and the
job does not run `--frozen`, so a drifted lock is rewritten rather than rejected. ⭐ Measured both
directions on a re-runnable control: no lock + an 11-h-old version required exactly is a hard error;
the same version reached through a **floating `^3`** range but present in a lock resolves under the
default policy. The lock had to be generated **by CI itself** — jsr.io, deno.land and esm.sh are all
403 from the sandbox — and was byte-verified against that output.

**E5 pass ten — two markers, and the third candidate was rejected on re-derivation.**
`panini-ingest` at **67% of a 60,000 ms wall** (3,501 runs) and `evm-transfers-ingest` at 44% with a
p90 of 25,536 ms. ⛔ `admin/backfill-offer-fill-sales` ranked next at 27% — but its `after()` path is
the LEGACY one, so that maximum belongs to the path that *cannot* be killed at the wall. **Rank by
margin, then check which path produced the number.** Below it everything is ≤20%, so the wall-margin
tier is exhausted.

**⭐ The one that matters most: the IPFS proxy was killing transfers it had already won.** One
production request carries the whole diagnosis — a **200**, `[ipfs-media] ok … elapsedMs=6037`, and then
**four uncaught `TimeoutError`s**. `AbortSignal.timeout(8_000)` starts at fetch time and stays live for
the response body, so a transfer whose headers arrived at 6 s was aborted mid-flight at 8 s, after the
success log had gone out. **426 of them across 60 users in 24 h.** ⚠ And it was invisible at the one
place that looks: the `ok` line records the DECISION to stream, not the outcome. Fixed as two phases on
one controller, plus a counting `TransformStream` so `ok` **with no** `streamed` is the signal — read by
correlation, exactly like the pipeline heartbeat marker.

**Then verifying THAT produced the fifth.** A probe returned `reason=transport name=Error` at
**7,800 ms** — which is either a real fault 200 ms inside the deadline or **our own abort with its
reason discarded by the runtime**, and those need opposite responses. The timing says the first (every
real abort in the logs sits at 7,982–7,999 ms), but ⚠ **the name stopped being a safe discriminator the
moment the code left `AbortSignal.timeout` behind**: a manual `controller.abort(reason)` preserves the
reason only in some runtimes, and this route ships to the edge. Now classified off
`controller.signal.aborted` — the route's own state, which no runtime can reinterpret.

⚠ **Two tests were pinning spellings rather than properties, and both were rewritten rather than
deleted.** The ipfs stubs used `body: "binarydata"` — a string, where `Response.body` is a stream in
every runtime this route ships to, so they passed while exercising a shape production never sees. And
"names an ABORT distinctly" rejected with a `TimeoutError`-named error and **never aborted anything**,
so it would have gone green on a route that called every failure an abort.

### The second half — and the pattern held: every one of these came out of checking the last

**The whole class, swept rather than the two files that failed.** 146 files bound a `fetch`; the subset
where a signal can kill a *response body* is a route that returns an upstream `.body` as a Response, and
a comment-stripped walk finds **exactly two**. The second, `avatar-media`, had the same shape unfixed —
plus a defect of its own: **every failure branch returned 502 with no log at all**, so *"our bound
fired"* and *"the host answered"* were spelled identically. That is the ambiguity that left ipfs-media's
soft-fail path unreachable dead code for months, and this route had inherited that file's TIMEOUT lesson
(its constant cites it) and not its OBSERVABILITY one. Population now zero, so it is a **ban**.

⛔ **And two sibling proxies had the half-bound version:** `badge-image` and `moment-thumbnail` read
their bodies with `await upstream.arrayBuffer()` **after** the try/catch closes. No live instance
claimed — they buffer, and the objects are small — and the code says so rather than implying a count.

**⭐ THE BIGGEST FIND CAME FROM VERIFYING A HEARTBEAT I HAD JUST ADDED.** The Vercel error table showed
`Task timed out after 60 seconds` on `cron/evm-transfers-ingest`, and the DB showed that tick with a
marker, a terminal row, `ok = true` and `duration_ms = 60,464` against a **60,000 ms** wall. **The
platform killed it and the correlation is clean** — the terminal write raced the wall and won. So
`heartbeat + terminal row -> ran to completion`, in this repo's doctrine since 08-20, is too strong: the
test answers *"did a row LAND"*, never *"did the invocation SURVIVE"*.

**Following that thread produced the night's best single fix.** Grouping Vercel's timeouts by
`requestPath`: **`wallet-backfill-golazos` 6 in 24 h — more than every other route on the platform
combined.** It uses `after()`, so a kill writes nothing; and it was invisible to the heartbeat ratchet
**by construction**, because that guard reads each ROUTE FILE for a `pipeline_runs` write and this
route's terminal write is one delegation away in `lib/chains/flow/wallet-backfill-helpers.ts`. Five
routes were outside it.

🚨 **Fourth instance of "a guard scoped to where the bug was found", and the instructive one — it had no
glob at all.** That ratchet went out of its way NOT to use a path convention; its header argues that
*"routes under `app/api/cron/` is a curated list wearing a glob"*. It was still scoped to the past,
because the structural predicate it chose asked about **the text it was reading** rather than the set
where the property is meaningful.

⭐ **And the wall was inherited, not chosen.** `1791e9083` created all four wallet enrichers at
`maxDuration = 60`; `d57349c5a` raised AllDay to **600** two days later and the others followed — this
one did not, then took on AllDay's own runner. Siblings today: 600 / 600 / 300, **zero kills between
them**. The pre-change distribution settles it: **max 59,801 ms of a 60,000 ms wall — 0.997 — over 1,621
runs**, p99 already at 78%. A maximum at 99.7% of the ceiling is the censored maximum in its purest
form.

**Then the instrument the doctrine correction asked for, step 1 of 3.** `lib/pipeline/route-walls.ts`
maps each route to its own `maxDuration` — 116 pairs — because the kill classifier had no per-route wall
to compare a duration against. ⛔ The cheap substitute (guess walls from round numbers) is **refuted
three of three**: the clustering above a round number is each route's INTERNAL budget *working*.
⭐ **The first sweep diagnoses its own errors:** a route cannot record a run much longer than its wall,
so `refresh-pack-grail-metrics-mv` at **2.7×** is the map failing — and migration `20260829235752` says
by name that the refresh **moved to pg_cron**, which the sweep spotted without being told.

⏳ **Owed, all three traffic-gated rather than broken** — the site was quiet from 00:40 PT:
`[ipfs-media] streamed` (no organic requests since deploy), `wallet-backfill-golazos-heartbeat` (its
last burst was 07:39Z, before the deploy) and `panini-ingest-heartbeat` (its caller is the residential
runner, idle). ✅ `evm-transfers-ingest-heartbeat` **is** verified live — five markers, all correlating.

## 2026-09-03 (nightly pass, cloud NO-PUSH) · a quiet honest night

Genuine overnight (01:02 PT, no clock skew). Push unavailable in the cloud runner (harvested pushurl
has no usable cred — Trevor's box + Claude Code push normally). Health GREEN: security clean ×4,
`detect_stalled_pipelines` [], the only trust breach the known declining structural
`unmapped_resolution_backlog_max`=209. The one new `medium` alert (`allday-pack-opens-backfill`) was a
90-min-window artifact over transient upstream Flow status-0 scan errors — interleaved ok ticks write
rows; benign. Accuracy gate checked and stable (per-day recompute HIGH/MED ratio 70–77% over 5 days;
the −194 standing HIGH+MED move is MEDIUM→LOW churn, not staleness). No artifacts flagged broken. Every

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · four re-derivations, and every one changed what the register said

A pass over the open register and the instrument fleet. **Nothing in this batch was a code fix — all
four were measurements that moved a filed conclusion**, which is the register's own stated purpose and
the thing that decays fastest.

**R29 — the three obvious levers for `job startup timeout` are all dead.** 13Z runs 117 timeouts in
1,171 ticks (10.0%) while 13 of 24 hours run zero, and ⭐ **the launcher's tick volume is flat across
all 24 hours**, so the band is not "more jobs firing". Re-staggering minutes is real but **2.3× against
a 15× hour effect**. Moving the victims is impossible — the top five run every 2–5 minutes. And the
load story **did not survive its own control**: the app-side proxy said independence outright, then the
DB-side control refused the tidy version, because **12Z is the second-heaviest hour on the instance
with two timeouts while 9Z is mid-range with sixty-five**. Load is neither sufficient nor necessary.

**D25 — the row was right and a population 37× larger had never been counted.** At the row's own grain
the count is 2,353 against its 66; split by key it is **62 on base editions — which IS the register's
number — and 2,291 on `::N` parallels**. ⭐ **For 895 of 895 offending parallel keys the largest serial
fits inside the BASE edition's circulation, zero exceptions**: the row carries the base moment's serial
and the denominator beside it is the parallel's run. Not corrupt data — a unit mismatch in the render.
⛔ And the obvious remedy is dead: copying `editions.circulation_count` over `wmc.mint_count` **fixes 2
of 899 keys.**

**R78 (new) — the two permanently-red workflows are one problem.** `edge-fn-drift` is red 30/30 **by
design** (5 drifted functions, 0 safe to redeploy, each with a dated reason), and the sentinel's
CRITICAL comes from **exactly one check** — Detector Health, counting that streak. ⭐ **The arm for
this hazard already existed and was firing correctly; nobody had joined the two badges.**

⛔ **And I corrected myself on it twenty minutes later.** I wrote that the two warns underneath "both
look like real work" — an inference from alert text. Measured: `allday-pack-opens-backfill` had
**self-cleared** (71 runs / 65 ok / 63 writing rows) and the unmapped backlog is `info`, draining at
**outflow 4,672 vs inflow 41 per day**. The finding survives; the colour I put on it did not.

⭐ **The pattern across all four: the mechanism was right every time and the ARITHMETIC or the GRAIN
was wrong.** ±5 s vs `>` earlier in the session; hour vs minute in R29; base vs parallel in D25; alert
text vs the numbers in R78. **None of these were caught by re-reading the filing — only by re-running
it with a control.**

ⓘ **A guard written this morning caught a real regression this evening.**
`api-error-copy-reaches-the-user-not-the-code` went **5 → 0** on `87a173c40`, because a concurrent
session wrote back a stale copy of `DashboardClient.tsx` and silently reverted the `apiErrorMessage`
fix. Restored in `4c6353c08`. ⚠ **That is the THIRD stale-copy clobber in one day** — the inbox
correction, the ledger, and now product source — and the only one caught by a machine was the one with
a count assertion pointed at it.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the obvious spelling of a correlation manufactured three kills, and only live data said so

Closed R11's structural residual: `detect_stalled_pipelines()` keyed on `max(started_at)` over a
pipeline's own terminal rows, so it could report silence and could not report **why** — and "the
schedule stopped" and "the route is killed at its wall before it can log" produce an identical
signature there while needing opposite responses. The evidence had existed since 2026-08-20 (the
`<pipeline>-heartbeat` marker, now on 21 of 87 watchlist rows) and nothing read it.

⭐ **The lesson is not the fix, it is the first draft.** Counting a marker as orphaned when
`started_at > last_run` is the obvious spelling and it is wrong: run against production it named
three pipelines as having orphaned markers, **all three healthy**. Their marker lands **2–14 ms
after** the terminal row's `started_at` — the two timestamps come from different clocks inside the
route — so a tick was counting its own heartbeat as evidence of its own death. With the ±5 s window
`lib/pipeline/heartbeat.ts` already documents: **3 → 0**.

Nothing about the mechanism was wrong. The arithmetic was, by 14 milliseconds, and a fixture would
never have contained that skew — it only exists because a real route writes two timestamps from two
places.

⚠ **A counter that reads zero has to be shown reading nonzero.** The same correlation over the full
73 h retention finds exactly one uncorrelated marker fleet-wide: `fmv-recalc`, 2026-09-01 00:15:52Z.
A real killed tick, invisible to every alert on this platform, and the counter sees it.

ⓘ The equivalence was proved the way this repo requires — one statement, one snapshot, the whole
population with the silence threshold forced so all 87 rows qualify: 87 vs 87, zero either way. Both
added LATERALs are unGROUPed aggregates, so the row set *cannot* move; the enrichment can never
become a silencer.

ⓘ And the sandbox CAN run the DB-invariant suite: `/usr/lib/postgresql/16/bin` is present, `initdb`
refuses to run as root, and `su postgres -c ...` into a scratch datadir works. 183 files green.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the ratchet that drove a 15-route workstream is blind to the property it exists to enforce

Advanced register E5 (BUDGET 40 → 37) by converting the three `after()` routes with the tightest
measured margin against their own walls — `cron/allday-lock-refresh-batch` (**71 of 73 ticks between
270,077 ms and 292,225 ms against a 300,000 ms wall**, worst 97.4%), `wallet-backfill` (watchlist
severity HIGH, max 87%) and `cron/populate-pinnacle-wmc-fmv` (max 77%, one RPC call).

⭐ **The finding is not the conversions.** `after-route-heartbeat-ratchet` has driven this workstream
since 2026-08-20 and is a STATIC read: it asserts the route CALLS `writeInvocationHeartbeat`. That is
satisfied by a call placed anywhere in the callback — **including after the work, where the marker
cannot survive the kill it exists to record.** The whole contract is the ORDERING, and nothing in the
suite was asserting it.

Proved rather than argued: moving the call after `runBatch` reddens the new ordering tests and **the
ratchet stays green**. It is the shape this repo keeps paying for — *a guard that asserts the PRESENCE
of a thing rather than the PROPERTY that makes it work*.

⚠ **Two separate recorders cannot see an order between them.** The shared route harness records
`rpcCalls` and `writes` in different arrays, so it could not express "the marker came first" at all;
the pins wrap `from().insert` and `rpc()` into ONE interleaved log instead.

⚠ And an arm written `if (firstWalk >= 0) expect(...)` was corrected before commit: a conditional
assertion goes vacuous the moment the fixture stops reaching that branch, while still reading as
coverage.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the inbox's content had no watcher, and the detector's two failed designs were both this repo's own lessons

Two sessions edited one inbox filing twenty minutes apart. The second rewrote it in place from a copy
read before the first landed, deleting an 84-line `## ⛔ CORRECTION` block — the block refuting the
filing's headline number. A human reading the diff caught it (`4ed563071` destroyed it, `2f1263240`
restored it). **Nothing in CI moved, and the response was a memory note.**

🚨 **All three existing inbox guards passed and none COULD have failed.** They ask which filings exist
and how many; a rewrite that keeps the file keeps the count. That is the ledger's own *diff the SET,
not the count* lesson (`2966c0a`, 356 → 356) sitting unlearned one directory over.

⭐ **The part worth keeping is the two designs that failed, each refuted by the REAL commits rather
than by invented markdown.**

| design | on the incident | on the repair |
|---|---|---|
| **v1** — every `## ` heading before must exist after | caught | **false-positive** — the repair legitimately RENAMED two sections while merging |
| **v2** — the after-file must still hold SOME correction heading | **missed** — the clobbering commit added its own correction, 1 → 1 | clean |
| **v3** — content fingerprint | 1 | 0 |

v1 is the 2026-08-22/23 shape where the ledger guard **punished compliance** with its own future-date
arm. v2 is count-blindness reproducing *inside the guard written about count-blindness*. Neither
would have been visible against synthetic fixtures: both look correct on paper, and both were killed
by three commits that already existed.

⚠ **Validating a CI guard by reading it is not validating it.** The whole step body was extracted from
`ci.yml` and executed in a throwaway clone at each real commit — the incident exits 1, the repair
exits 0, an unrelated push exits 0 with "nothing to compare", a deleted filing exits 1, the
`[inbox-correction-retracted]` escape exits 0, a missing detector exits 1. Two bugs only that found:
a `… | while read` loop runs in a **subshell**, so the counters were discarded at the `done` and the
step would have reported `inspected: 0` whatever it found; and the removal case printed the CLOBBER
remedy, which cannot work for a deletion — **an error whose remedy fails costs the guard its
authority**, so the remedy is now gated on the failure it fits.

ⓘ Corrections only, deliberately: highest value, lowest churn, and *"the correction vanished"* has no
legitimate cause. Everything else in a filing stays free to be rewritten, which is what keeps the
guard from being one people switch off.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · two "raw slug reaches the user" findings, two DIFFERENT correct answers

Drained the last code item of the QA handoff's #9: `DashboardClient` threw `data.error`, so a user who
hit the wallet cap saw the literal string `plan_limit_reached`.

The route answers that rejection with BOTH a machine code and human copy — *"Free plan supports 3
saved wallets. Remove the wallet you have saved, or upgrade to RPC Pro."* — plus an `upgrade_url`.
⭐ **The sentence someone wrote for exactly this moment sat unused in the same response**, and the
actionable half never appeared at the one point it was relevant. The copy existed, was correct, and
reached nobody.

⚠ **The handoff named two line numbers; the expression was at five.** Fixing only what was reported
is how a class survives its own fix, so the guard asserts the count is **5** rather than asserting
that "the reported one" changed.

⭐ **The part worth keeping is that this and the `/login?error=` fix an hour earlier LOOK like the
same defect and are not.** Both present as "a raw slug reaches the user". But:

| | source of the value | correct fix |
|---|---|---|
| `/login?error=` | the QUERY STRING — attacker-controlled | allowlist; never render an unknown value |
| `DashboardClient` | our own API's RESPONSE BODY | restore the DISCARDED field |

An allowlist on the dashboard would have been wrong (most routes legitimately put human text in
`error`); preferring `message` on the login page would have been wrong (there is no `message`, and
the value is hostile). **Matching on the symptom would have produced one right answer and one wrong
one.** The discriminator is where the string came from, and it is invisible from the rendered output
— which is the only place the two findings were ever compared.

ⓘ The no-change control mattered here more than usual: without it, "prefer `message`" could have
silently downgraded every route that sends only `error` — a regression dressed as a fix, in the same
commit that claims to improve error copy.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the same disguise, twice in one session, in unrelated code — which is the finding

Took another P2 from the QA handoff: *"`/auth/confirm` ignores its `?redirect=`"*. The functional half
is exactly as filed — `/api/auth/request-magic-link` already carries the login page's `next` into the
emailed callback, and `AuthConfirmClient` never read it, hard-coding `router.replace("/")`. It only
LOOKED fine because `/` bounces a signed-in user to `/dashboard`, so the common case worked and every
deep link lost its destination silently.

🚨 **The half that was not filed:** the only sanitiser in the chain was `redirect.startsWith("/")`.
That is not a same-origin test — `//evil.example/x` starts with `/` and resolves to
`https://evil.example/x`. The shape was already baked into the emailed link, so honouring
`?redirect=` without fixing it would have **completed** the exploit: a link that genuinely is ours,
genuinely signs the victim in, and then lands them on someone else's page already authenticated and
primed to trust it.

⭐ **This is the SECOND time tonight I have found `startsWith("/")` used as a security check** — the
first was trophy-slab art a few hours earlier, in unrelated code, reached by a completely different
route (an OG-layer sweep, not the handoff). Two independent discoveries of one shape in one session
is not a coincidence to note in passing; it is the finding. **The lesson is not "remember to check
for `//`" — that is the fix. The lesson is that `startsWith("/")` READS like a same-origin check and
is not one**, which is precisely why it survives review, and why both instances sat in code that had
otherwise been carefully written.

⚠ **The fix had its own trap, and a test caught it rather than production:** `params` inside that
effect is the HASH — Supabase's implicit flow puts the token in the fragment — while `?redirect=` is
a QUERY param. Reading it from `params` would have been silently always-null and the whole fix inert
while every test still passed. It reads `window.location.search` instead, which also sidesteps the
Suspense boundary `useSearchParams` would have required.

ⓘ **Twice tonight my test asserted something the code got right, and I kept the correction in the
test.** A trailing CRLF is `.trim()`ed to a valid path, and a space is not a control character — it
cannot split a header and a browser percent-encodes it. Banning either would reject a legitimate
redirect for no security gain. Recorded in place so the next reader does not "fix" them back.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the handoff filed it as polish; the mechanism was a phishing surface on the login page

`docs/handoff-2026-09-02-onboarding-trophy-case-qa.md` #9 lists, among a batch of P2 polish, *"raw
error slugs reach users: `LoginClient.tsx:36-42` shows `auth_failed` / `missing_token` /
`session_failed` verbatim"*. The slug half IS polish. The mechanism underneath it is not, and reading
the code rather than the finding is what surfaced it.

`LoginClient` mapped two known values and **fell through to the raw query-string value for everything
else**. That value is attacker-supplied. So
`/login?error=Your+account+was+locked.+Call+555-0100+to+restore+it` rendered that sentence inside our
own error banner, in our voice, on the login page. React escapes it — this is not XSS — which in this
particular spot makes it worse, not better: no browser warning, no broken markup, just Rip Packs City
appearing to tell someone their account is locked, on the one page where they expect exactly that
kind of message.

⭐ **The defect was never the ugly slug. It was that an UNKNOWN value was rendered at all** — which is
why the fix is an allowlist with a generic fallback and not a prettifier. A prettifier would have
satisfied the filing and left the vector open.

⛔ **AND AN EXISTING TEST WAS PINNING THE DEFECT, with its own counter-example one case above it.**
`component-LoginClient` had:

- *"allowlist_unavailable is translated, never echoed as a raw code"* — asserts the right property
- *"forwards an /auth/confirm failure code into the inline error"* — asserts
  `getByText("session_failed")`, i.e. **requires** the raw slug to reach the user

Two adjacent cases asserting opposite things about the same banner, and the wrong one was written to
sound like a feature. Inverted rather than deleted, per the standing rule: the property it is named
for (a confirm failure reaches the inline error) is unchanged; only the spelling moved.

⚠ The guard includes a source walk over `AuthConfirmClient` and `api/auth/callback` that extracts
every `?error=` value they set and fails if one is missing from the map — asserting the walk found ≥3
so it cannot pass vacuously. A slug added later without copy degrades to the generic fallback, which
is safe but vaguer than it needs to be; this makes that visible instead of silent.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · took the handoff's #7 and the re-derivation was worth more than the fix

`docs/handoff-2026-09-02-onboarding-trophy-case-qa.md` #7: *"Trophy pins are entirely
client-asserted"* — `POST /api/profile/trophy` upserts `playerName`, `fmv`, `serialNumber`, `tier`,
`thumbnailUrl` straight from the body, so "a campaign that says 'show off your case' invites a
$1,000,000 LeBron #1/1 on someone's public page within a day." Recommendation: resolve every display
field server-side.

⛔ **True of the WRITE path, and ~80% false of the product.** `get_trophy_slab_data` renders public
slabs with `COALESCE(e.<field>, tm.<field>)` and `COALESCE(f.fmv_usd, tm.fmv)` — the live `editions`
and `fmv_snapshots` values WIN, so a forged player, set, tier, circulation, video, FMV or badge list
is overridden the moment the edition resolves. ⭐ **Reading the handler says twelve fields are
forgeable. Reading the RENDERER says two are.** Implementing the filing as written would have been
mostly redundant work on the most-shared surface in the product.

**The two that are real**, because they are not COALESCEd: `thumbnail_url` — rendered publicly AND
fetched server-side by `/api/og/profile/[username]`, which inlines trophy art as data URIs, so an
arbitrary value is an arbitrary image on a public page *plus* a server-side fetch of a chosen host —
and `serial_number`, because "#1 of 15,000" is the entire claim a trophy makes.

⭐ **The allowlist is derived, not guessed.** Seven hosts straight out of `editions.thumbnail_url`
with their row counts, plus the same-origin `/api/public/pinnacle-image/…` proxy that a host-only
list would have rejected and that one live row uses. A guessed list silently blanks the art of
whichever host it forgot, and a blank slab explains nothing.

⚠ **Said plainly rather than implied: this is not an incident.** 19 rows, 7 users, every thumbnail
legitimate. ⭐ **And the no-change control is the part I would want a reviewer to check:** all 19 pins
resolve in `wallet_moments_cache` and every stored serial ALREADY agrees with the indexed one — so
the change alters no live value, *and* the new read is demonstrably not a dead path. Both halves
matter; either alone would leave the fix unproven in a different direction.

**The mutant worth remembering** is the protocol-relative URL: `//evil.example/x.png` resolves to
`https://evil.example/x.png` in a browser, so the obvious "starts with `/` means same-origin" check
waves it straight through. That is why the same-origin branch is a PREFIX test on
`/api/public/`, not a slash test — and why the case is in the suite rather than in a comment.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · drained the QA handoff's P0 — and the fix was already in the tree, one route over, for the fourth time today

`docs/handoff-2026-09-02-onboarding-trophy-case-qa.md` is a real new-user walkthrough that shipped
nothing and handed off findings in campaign-priority order. Took **#1 (P0)**, re-deriving it first per
the standing rule: both of its numbers hold (`wallet_usernames` = **9,370**, exactly as filed).

`POST /api/profile/resolve-and-associate` called `resolveTopShotUsername()` — Top Shot's public GQL
and nothing else — so during an outage every username signup 502'd, including for the 9,370 handles we
already had. The filing's 72 h counts of `Top Shot GraphQL failed with 530` were `/api/wallet-search`
243, this route 43, `offers-sweep` 108: intermittent for days.

⭐ **`resolveTopShotUsernameCacheAware` — four DB cache layers, live GQL last, write-back on a live
hit — was already in the tree, used by the ANONYMOUS home analyzer**, and the filing confirmed it
resolving `jamesdillonbond` during the same outage this route was failing on. The signed-in path, the
one the hero copy leads with, had the worse implementation. **Fourth "a correct sibling is not a
guard" today**, after `fetchFmvBatch`, `slabsError` and `og/profile`. Four in one session is not four
coincidences — it is what happens when a fix is applied where the bug was reported instead of where
the SHAPE lives.

**The handoff didn't name the second half, and it is the worse one.** The live resolver returned
`null` for both "Top Shot says no such user" and "we couldn't reach Top Shot", so a cache miss during
an outage rendered as **"Couldn't find that Dapper username"** — sending a real person away believing
their own handle was wrong. The outcome union carries the reason, so those are now 404 and 502. The
honesty canon's *a failed read is not an empty result*, on a signup form.

⚠ **And I added the mirror image rather than inheriting it:** `empty_username` now returns the route's
existing 400. Reporting the user's bad input as OUR outage is the same error pointed the other way,
and a whitespace-only handle reaches that branch because the guard above only rejects a missing field.

⚠ **The source-level pin is what makes the fix testable at all.** The resolver is mocked in these
tests, so a route reverted to the live-only function passes every behavioural case. Without an
assertion on WHICH resolver is called, the guard would have been green over the bug.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · a social card was 16.4x low on a dollar figure, and the two halves of the bug were individually invisible

Swept the OG layer — CLAUDE.md's fourth honesty layer, and the one I had not looked at. It came back
almost clean: `og/profile` and `og/share` both carry an explicit `ok`/`fetched` flag with comments
explaining why, and the `?? 0` sites elsewhere are per-row field defaults inside successful reads, not
population zeros. One card was not clean.

`/api/og/insights/panini-squeeze` published **"$143,849 still sealed in packs" beside "4,813
editions"**. The true sum is **$2,358,840**. It read
`.select("sealed_fmv_exposure_usd", { count: "exact" })` with no bound and summed in JS: PostgREST
caps an unbounded `.select()` at 1,000 rows and reports no error, while `count: "exact"` is a real
`COUNT(*)` over the whole view.

⛔ **Neither half is visible on its own.** The count is right. The sum is a correct sum of the rows it
was given. Nothing errors — supabase-js resolves rather than throwing, and the cap does not even set
`error`, so the read SUCCEEDS and returns a fifth of the table. **It is the PAIRING that lies:** an
exact count next to a truncated sum reads as one measurement, and on a social card the number travels
without the page around it.

⭐ **The lesson of the evening arrived again in the guard, twice.** A mutant that deleted the
deterministic `.order("id")` SURVIVED my first test, then survived the fix to that test:

1. First pass: the stub sliced its array positionally regardless of ordering, so the missing order
   was unobservable.
2. Second pass: I modelled physical order by rotating the table between calls — but the route calls
   `.from()` once **per page**, so a counter scoped inside `from()` reset every page and the table
   never appeared to move.

**Both times the guard looked right and measured nothing.** And the case only works with DISTINCT
per-row values: with a constant value the overlaps and omissions cancel exactly, so a wrong row SET
produces the right SUM. That cancellation is the whole reason unordered `.range()` paging survives
review — it is invisible to every count-based check, and it was nearly invisible to a test written
specifically to catch it.

⚠ **And `og/profile` is the third "correct sibling" today.** Its own comment records this defect —
*"a failed `saved_wallets` read returned `[]`, `totalFmv` reduced to 0, and the card published $0 as
that person's portfolio"* — fixed there 2026-08-13, while the profile PAGE rendering the same two
numbers kept the bug until tonight. Someone fixed the share card and never checked the page it
shares.

**Sweep closed with a number:** 59 files use `count: "exact"`; **0** now pair an unbounded `.select()`
with a JS `reduce`.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the fifth honesty layer, found by sweeping SEEDS rather than FILES — and the file-level sweep was worthless, which is the transferable part

Swept `app/` for server-seeded props (`initial*={…}`) looking for the class CLAUDE.md describes as the
one its four-helper table does not cover: a seed that arrives as `[]` on a failed read carrying no
provenance.

⛔ **My first sweep flagged six files and was wrong about all six.** It asked "does this FILE contain
failure vocabulary" — and `player/[slug]`, `set/[slug]` and `series/[slug]` all gate their seeds with
`editionsOk` + `SectionUnavailable`, a spelling my regex didn't know. Widening the vocabulary then
produced **zero** offenders across all 32 seeded pages, which is the real finding: **every server page
that seeds a client contains some failure vocabulary, so a file-scoped grep cannot discriminate at
all.** The property is per-SEED. Both defects I eventually found are in files that handle failure
correctly a few lines away. ⭐ **"Fix per PANEL, not per page" has a sweep-side twin: SEARCH per
panel, not per page** — and my own guard nearly repeated the exact "assert the property, not the
spelling" mistake it exists to catch.

**What that turned up.**

1. **`/profile/<username>`** — `page.tsx` computed `result.ok` and **discarded it**, seeding
   `initialWallets={[]}` on failure. The KPI tiles render `PORTFOLIO FMV —` / `MOMENTS —` and the
   saved-wallet list does not render at all when empty, so a collector who added six wallets sees a
   page with none. ⚠ It is the rendering **this page's own header records as the 2026-06-12 audit
   finding**, re-created for the failure path once the SSR seed fixed it for the loading path. ⭐ And
   `slabsError` — the identical distinction, with a comment explaining why it was necessary — sits
   **twenty lines below** the wallet fetch that lacked it. A correct sibling is not a guard, at
   twenty lines.

2. **Seven public `/insights` boards** stamped `Updated <render clock>` unconditionally, each one
   directly above a degraded notice it already passed correctly. "Updated just now" over a board that
   failed to load is the fabricated-number family applied to a timestamp.

⚠ **The test had to be `renderToString`, and mutation proved it:** ProfileClient refetches on mount,
so in jsdom both `useState(true)` and `useState(false)` leave every client assertion green. Only the
SSR HTML — what crawlers, unfurls and the pre-hydration paint get, and what ISR caches for 300 s —
carries the difference.

⭐ **Mutation found a defect in my own fix that no test I had written would have.** Removing
`setProfileError(false)` from the success path survived everything: the notice would have persisted
on a profile that had loaded perfectly, because I had only tested that failures show it, never that
successes clear it. **Over-correcting is its own defect and needs its own case.**

**And a third instance came from reading the logs for the fix I had just shipped.** Production at
00:12Z: `AD FMV map size: 6190` — my new read, healthy — immediately followed by
`get_allday_sniper_deals error: canceling statement due to statement timeout`, a different read
reached only because the All Day GraphQL is 403ing. That path already named its source (I had added
that hours earlier), but its return stamped `lastRefreshed: new Date()`, and `SniperStatsBar` renders
"updated <time>" from exactly that field — so the degraded, empty board claimed it had just been
refreshed. ⭐ **Verifying a fix is a better finder of the next defect than looking for one**, and the
same freshness shape turned up in three unrelated places in one evening once I knew to look for it.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · two sessions measured the same swap and disagreed by 15× — both harnesses were wrong, and the real number is the one that justifies finishing the job

A concurrent session shipped `get_editions_latest_fmv()` for the concierge with a headline of **249×
fewer buffers**. I had, hours earlier, shipped the sniper-feed read that CHUNKS AT EXACTLY 500 — the
size their "before" arm measured as catastrophic. Two possibilities: my fix was a regression, or one
of the measurements was not measuring production. Testing the curve was not optional.

**Neither number was production.** Cost is linear in the id count in every pushed-down form (40 /
200 / 500 ids → 2,894 / 14,624 / 35,852 buffers, ~72 each), so **chunk size is a round-trip decision,
not a cost decision** and my fix was not a regression. But their 1.33M was reproducible — from a
`WITH ids AS (… ORDER BY external_id LIMIT 500)` + `IN (SELECT id FROM ids)`, which the planner
serves with a **hash** semi-join over the fully materialised view. Change that one ORDER BY to `id`
and the same query is a **merge** semi-join at 152,869 buffers. 8.7×, one word. And PostgREST sends
neither: `pg_stat_statements` (`query LIKE 'WITH pgrst_source%'`) shows `edition_id = ANY($1)`, a
bound array that becomes an index condition with no join to pick a strategy for.

⭐ **The lesson is not "they were wrong."** My own half was the mirror image: I had written
"`IN (<literal uuids>)` ← **what PostgREST sends**" into `database.md` as a table row, and never
checked. It happens to plan identically, so the conclusion survived — but **a claim that is right by
luck is indistinguishable from one that is right by measurement until someone tests it, and the cost
of the difference lands on whoever inherits it.** The instrument for this took one query, against a
table that was already recording the answer.

**The correction that mattered more than the ratio.** Both of us framed this as pushdown/no-pushdown.
The real mechanism: `edition_id = ANY(…)` DOES reach the partitioned index and STILL costs ~70
buffers per edition, because the view has **no per-group LIMIT** — it reads every snapshot row for
each edition (~35 and growing) and `Unique` discards all but the newest. The LATERAL's `LIMIT 1`
costs ~4. 👉 **"Reaches the index" is not "is cheap."** Under the pushdown framing, a read keyed on
the DISTINCT ON column is *done*; under the right one it is 17× from done. That framing is why my
own sweep bullet the same morning called every keyed app caller **"Clean"** — a word that closed a
question the measurement had not answered.

**Shipped on the back of it.** `/api/sniper-feed`'s All Day leg moved to the RPC: 424,475 → ~26,000
buffers per request. The earlier fix took it from ~1.84M to 424k and looked finished. ⭐ **A fix that
is correct, measured, documented and 4× can read as complete when it captured under a quarter of the
win.**

**And the guards were vacuous in the way this repo keeps re-learning.** Both pins for that read
asserted `.from("fmv_current")` and `.range(` against the WHOLE FILE. The All Day leg stopped using
the view — and both still matched, on the Top Shot leg and the jersey read. A case *titled* for All
Day would have passed with the All Day read deleted. ⚠ **A file-scoped assertion about a function is
a vacuous assertion waiting for the function to move.** Both now slice `computeAllDaySniperFeed`; the
chunk pin asserts the VALUE is ≤1000 rather than the constant's spelling, because raising it to 6,190
would satisfy every other pattern and silently truncate.

**Then the correction paid for itself twice.** Getting the mechanism right — *a qual on the DISTINCT
ON key becomes an index condition; anything else builds the whole view* — gave a property to sweep
for that the pushdown framing did not. Two live instances, in code a sweep the same morning had
cleared:

- **`/api/overview-stats`** counted HIGH-confidence editions with `.eq(collection_id).eq(confidence)`
  on the view: **1,331,923 buffers / 14,085 ms**, against 909 / 39 ms on `edition_fmv_current`, the
  materialised table that exists for exactly this. ⚠ **It has no caller** — nothing in the repo
  fetches it, production logged zero requests in 72h — so it is a landmine, not a wait. Fixed rather
  than deleted because the next person to wire an overview panel would inherit the worst
  `fmv_current` shape in the repo, already written and looking reviewed.
- **`/api/support-chat`'s `get_edition_listings`** filtered the view through an embedded
  `editions!inner(external_id)`: **933,871 buffers / 1,390 ms** inside the 60 s concierge lambda, on
  every "what is this worth" turn. The edition UUID was in hand two statements above. Keyed on it:
  **6 buffers / 0.06 ms**. It was also less CORRECT — the embed matched `external_id` with no
  collection scope, and Top Shot stores every moment twice.

⭐ **The morning sweep missed both because it enumerated the columns that are WRONG.** `collection_id`
beside `fmv_current` is greppable; an embedded-resource filter is not — the offending column is not
in the read's own table. The new guard asserts the RIGHT column is PRESENT instead
(`__tests__/fmv-current-reads-are-keyed-on-edition-id.test.ts`, a tree walk, ban at zero, asserting
the count it inspected). **A ban list is only ever as long as the last incident.**

⚠ **One instrument caveat worth keeping:** `pg_stat_statements` is at **4,905 of 5,000** entries, so
it is evicting. It was the right tool for reading what PostgREST actually sends, and it is NOT a
tool for concluding a query never ran.

**And a third measurement of the same view landed mid-flight, refuted the same way.** An inbox filing
reported *"THREE ids cost the same 1.33M buffers as five hundred — the cost is FIXED, not
proportional"*. The curve, both forms: 1 / 3 / 10 / 100 / 500 ids → **6 / 23 / 311 / 5,312 / 25,330**
buffers. A straight line. ⛔ Its one-line discriminator — *"`.eq` is fine, `.in` is not"* — cleared
`/api/overview-stats` and `/api/support-chat` as "safe, need nothing"; those were the two at 1.33M and
934k. **A wrong axis does not merely mis-rank: it hands the next session a reason to skip the actual
defects.** Corrected in the filing and INDEX.md rather than archived — the site list, the drop-in RPC
and "enumerate consumers by the OBJECT, not by the language the first instances were written in" all
survive, and that last rule is exactly what would have caught the two it cleared.

⭐ **Three sessions measured one view in one day and produced 249×, "fixed cost", and 17×.** The two
wrong answers came from harnesses, and the tell was identical in both: **an arm far worse than the
same query issued the production way.** The cheapest defence is one query against
`pg_stat_statements` for what the client actually sends — which none of the three ran before
publishing, this session included.

**Last thing measured, and it inverted the framing I had been working inside all evening.** Having
fixed three sites, the obvious next move was a wider helper so the remaining thirteen `.in()` sites
could convert too. Ranked them by what they actually cost first (`pg_stat_statements` since its
2026-08-12 reset): the ONE collection-scoped shape cost **31,052,866 blocks in 43 calls**; **every
id-list call in the product combined** cost **8,691,506 over 4,443**. ⭐ **3.6× the buffers from 1% of
the calls, out of a single file. A file count is not a cost model — and "sixteen routes" was a file
count.** No migration; the remaining sites convert when a wider helper exists for another reason.

⚠ **And I had made the same mistake I spent the evening correcting in others.** I wrote
"`wallet-search` on a 5,000-moment wallet is ~350k buffers where ~20k would do" into `database.md`,
the ledger and the inbox as though it were sized. It was a projection. Observed: **2,042 blocks/call,
~29 editions.** The tail exists — over 1,165 wallets, distinct editions run p50 50 · p90 3,196 · p99
7,674 · max 11,349 — so a p90 wallet does cost ~224k. But "the tail is real" and "this is what the
route costs" are different claims, and I published the second while holding evidence for only the
first. **Writing a projected number in the same paragraph as measured ones launders it into a
measurement.** Corrected in all three places.

⚠ One instrument note that cuts both ways: `pg_stat_statements` is at **4,905 of 5,000 entries** and
therefore evicting. It was the right tool for reading what PostgREST actually sends — the thing none
of the three sessions checked before publishing — and every total it gives here is a LOWER BOUND, so
it is not a tool for concluding a query never ran.


## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · confirmed the daytime monitor's two pg_cron items — one classification was wrong, both were already fixed

The `rpc-daytime-monitor` filing (15:10Z) asked the night pass to confirm two failures on the next
tick. Both settle now, because both are things this session shipped hours before the filing was
written.

**1. `rpc-topshot-onchain-rekey` permission denial — resolved, and the filing's reasoning was right.**
The grant migration is named `20260902113501`; the failed run was 11:33:00Z. ⚠ But
`supabase_migrations.schema_migrations` has **no applied-at column** — `version, statements, name,
created_by, idempotency_key, rollback` — so the filename is the only clock and that is corroboration,
not proof. ⭐ **The better evidence is the instrument, not the timestamp:**
`check_cron_heavy_job_exec_drift()` returns `{"inspected": 56, "offenders": []}`. That function
exists for exactly the recurrence the filing asks someone to watch for, walks all 56 cron_heavy
callables rather than the one that failed, and is a hard smoke arm. **A reset would now red the smoke
suite within the hour instead of surfacing as one failed job a day later.**

**2. `rpc-refresh-challenge-costs` — the classification is wrong.** The filing called it "a single
statement-timeout after two clean days → saturation collateral, self-clearing". `cron.job_run_details`
over eleven consecutive daily runs: **four failures (08-23, 08-24, 08-26, 09-02) — 36%**, with the
successes trending 57 → 74 → 81 → **101** s against a 120 s ceiling. ⚠ **The filing looked back two
days, and the two days it sampled (40.1 s, 40.5 s) were the two fastest runs in the window.** A rate
needs a distribution, not a snapshot. Already fixed: migration `20260902120329` hoists arm 1 out of
the per-row COALESCE, verified live in `pg_proc`, and measured **1.208 s**. ⭐ Credit to the filing —
it named the correct lever ("the same DISTINCT-ON-filtered-on-non-key-column shape") without knowing
it had landed.

## …which prompted sweeping every DISTINCT ON view, and the negative result is the interesting part

21 views use `DISTINCT ON`. **Every app/lib caller filters on the view's own key. Of the 8 SQL
functions reading `fmv_current`, every one is either keyed on `edition_id` or has ALREADY been
converted to a LATERAL — with the reason written into the source:** *"the planner cannot push a join
key into DISTINCT ON, so every call walked all 1.31M fmv_snapshots rows"*.

⭐ **So the SQL layer learned this lesson thoroughly and the TypeScript layer never heard it.** Three
functions carry explicit comments about this exact pushdown; the route-side read I fixed today was
making the identical mistake with no comment anywhere in `app/`. **A lesson can be fully absorbed in
one layer of a codebase and completely absent from another** — "a correct sibling is not a guard",
one level up. When you fix a query-shape bug, ask which other LANGUAGE in the repo issues that query.

⭐ **And there was already a materialised answer I did not know about:** `edition_fmv_current`, a real
table refreshed hourly, carrying `collection_id` — so the collection-scoped read has a supported home.
Five functions already use it. My chunked `.in()` fix is still right for the sniper (it wants live
FMV, not hourly), but "all FMVs for collection X" should read that table.

⚠ **My screen was wrong twice before it was right.** `'%fmv_current%'` also matches
`edition_fmv_current`, so 8 real view-readers looked like 13 and two functions came back as confident
false positives. Word boundaries (`prosrc ~ '\mfmv_current\M'`) fixed it. Same family as the
substring-vs-line-start rule the ledger already has.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · 🚨 I fixed a route that does not run — and post-deploy verification is what caught it

Correcting my own work from earlier today. The Golazos half of the pack-EV fix is **overstated: the
route it fixes has not run since 2026-08-27.**

`app/api/cron/compute-laliga-pack-ev/route.ts` has **zero rows in `pipeline_runs`** over the whole
~73 h retention window — not even a heartbeat — and `pipeline_runs_daily` (indefinite) puts its last
run at 2026-08-27. It was superseded by a Supabase **edge function**: pg_cron jobid 44
`rpc-compute-golazos-pack-ev`, `37 */6 * * *`, active, calling
`/functions/v1/compute-golazos-pack-ev`. ⛔ **And the live edge function does not have the defect at
all** — it *writes* `pack_drop_pool` per dist and computes EV by iterating the ~40 distributions it
already holds from the upstream GraphQL (`rows_found: 40`, never ~1,000). No unbounded pool scan
exists on the live path.

⭐ **"Name the caller before you touch the function" — and I did not.** I read the code, measured the
table (1,958 rows / 211 dists / 134 reachable — all true), wrote four behavioural tests,
mutation-tested them 4/4, and never asked whether the route executes. CLAUDE.md's own example for
this rule is an afternoon spent on a function with zero callers. **The check is one query** —
`SELECT count(*) FROM pipeline_runs WHERE pipeline = '<name>'` — **and it belongs before the fix.**
I am adding it to my own pre-flight for anything under `app/api/cron/`.

⭐ **What caught it was VERIFYING the fix, not reviewing it.** I went to confirm a before/after in
`pack_ev_history` and the post-fix run had written *fewer* dists (23 vs 36–39). Chasing that
disagreement is what surfaced `rows_found: 40` and the absence of my own new `extra.pool_rows_read` —
i.e. the runs were never mine. **A post-deploy check that can only confirm is not a check.** Had the
number gone up I would have written "verified" and been wrong.

⚠ **The measurement I first reached for was also the wrong subject.** `pack_ev_history` rows grouped
by minute looked like "runs of my pipeline"; they were another producer's. Same family as pairing a
count from one table with a property from another — I compared MY code against SOMEONE ELSE'S output
and read the delta as a regression.

What stands: the ownership-walk half of that entry is real and live. The Golazos change is harmless
and strictly more correct — correct dead code now. ⚠ **Not deleted:** no pg_cron job and no in-repo
caller reference it, but cron-job.org and Trevor's Task Scheduler are invisible from this sandbox and
CLAUDE.md names both as real caller sources, so a five-of-eight sweep is not grounds for deletion.
Filed as a dead-code candidate instead.

**The #57 sweep's true live-defect count is 3, not 4.** Corrected in the ledger and in the register
entry, at the point a reader actually lands.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · re-derived an inbox filing and RECOMMENDED AGAINST one of its own fixes

Picked up the `sales-serial-backfill` filing from this morning's cloud session — it names two halves,
an edge-side one (operator-gated, can't touch) and a DB-side one it explicitly gated on a measurement
it hadn't taken. Took the measurement. **Nothing shipped, and that is the result.**

⭐ **The DB-side escalating cooldown must not be built: its population is ZERO.** Joining every
failure row back to its sale, **all 2,069 AllDay `not_in` rows (and all 296 `no_holder` rows) have
since had their serial resolved by another path**, so the picker's `WHERE serial_number IS NULL`
already excludes every one of them. The proposed change would act on an empty set.

⭐ **And the filing's *premise* is refuted, not just its urgency.** It reasoned that "`not_in` means
the moment is not in the recorded holder's collection, which for a sold-on moment is PERMANENT."
100% of them recovered. `not_in` is a statement about one stale holder snapshot, not about the moment.

⭐ **This is the good side of "a filed DECISION NOT TO ACT is also a hypothesis."** That filing
declined to ship pending a measurement and wrote its own falsifier — *"an escalating backoff on a
recoverable row just delays the recovery."* Shipping it would have delayed recovery on 2,365 rows
that were all going to resolve. The conservative call was right, and now there is evidence rather
than caution.

⚠ **Two of the filing's numbers do not reproduce, both from mixing populations.** "About 14 attempts
per row" — `max(retry_count)` across the whole lane is **6**, mean **2.07**. The 14 came from
dividing an attempt SUM over the failures table by a row count that had already shrunk as rows
resolved: **a count from one population against a property of another**, which is a rule this repo
already has. And the `http_429` "since" date is 2026-08-03 live, not the 2026-08-29 filed.

The falsifier itself FIRED in the other direction: the Top Shot lane is still failing (682 → 1,288
rows on `http_530` in eleven hours, newest failure an hour before I looked), so that half is open,
edge-side, and stays queued. Worth noting it is *not* hopeless — 38% of the 530-failed rows and 55%
of the 429-failed rows have resolved since, which argues for the breaker (stop burning the batch
while the origin is down) rather than for abandoning the retry.

Wrote all of it back into the filing so the next session — or the nightly pass, which drains this
directory — cannot pick up the withdrawn fix.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the 45-second sniper timeout was a DISTINCT ON pushdown, found by correlating two errors

The one open item from this morning's runtime-error triage, now root-caused and fixed.

⭐ **The diagnosis came from a CORRELATION, not from either error.** `AD fmv_current error @0:
canceling statement due to statement timeout` and `Vercel Runtime Timeout Error: Task timed out after
45 seconds` share a **deployment and a second** — same request, both ceilings hit. Neither line alone
says anything about the other; the pairing is the whole finding. Worth doing routinely: group runtime
errors by `lastDeployment` + timestamp before treating them as separate problems.

`fmv_current` is `DISTINCT ON (edition_id)`. The read filtered on `collection_id`, and **a qual on the
DISTINCT ON key pushes down while a qual on any other column does not**. One page measured
**274,519 rows walked, 263,392 buffers, 19.5 s** — times six pages, against a 45 s budget. ~136 s of
DB work for one user request.

Fixed by inverting the order: editions first (keyset on the indexed `id`), then `.in("edition_id", …)`
on `fmv_current`.

⚠ **I nearly banked the wrong number.** My first probe used `IN (SELECT id FROM …)` and got a merge
semi-join: 114,446 buffers / 287 ms. That is 68× better and it *looks* like the fix — but PostgREST
sends a **literal array**, not a subquery, and that is a different plan. Measuring the real shape (a
40-uuid literal list, small enough to paste) gave `Index Cond: (edition_id = ANY (...))` on all three
partitions at **2,894 buffers / 21 ms**, ~72 buffers per edition. **A control must use the production
caller** — I know that rule and still reached for the convenient form first.

⭐ **The right pattern was one function away the whole time.** `fetchFmvBatch` in the same file has
always chunked `fmv_current` by `.in("edition_id", …)` for the Top Shot leg. **A correct sibling is
not a guard.** Third time today a fix applied per-file left a sibling behind (after the `support-report`
NaN guard and the badge/jersey caps).

⚠ **A control I wrote THIS MORNING had to be inverted, and the rule is the point.** It pinned
`if (page.length < FMV_PAGE) break;` — the *spelling* of the loop I then deliberately deleted. The
property it was protecting (the map is bounded, never one unbounded read) still holds and holds
better, so the case is inverted rather than deleted and now pins the property, with `collection_id`
banned from that read. **Pin the property, not the spelling** — broken within hours of applying it.

⚠ **The no-progress cursor guard survived the first mutation sweep for the third time today.** No
fixture produces "the same full page forever" unless you build one; it took a mock flag that ignores
the cursor. **A guard against a state your fixtures cannot reach is untested by construction** — that
is now three separate paging loops where I had to add the flag or the case deliberately.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · 🚨 I had main red for four commits, and the instrument said green

Worth writing up plainly: **CI failed on every code commit I pushed today** — `24aed0c69`,
`cbef9d016`, `1b8f36065`, `bd7ab8dad` — and I only found out because I went to check at the end.
Last green before mine was `6d3763bd6`. Fixed and verified green.

One job, the same one each time: **ESLint ratchet**, `no-unused-vars grew 353 -> 355`, from two
unused mock parameters in a test file I created this morning. Every other job passed on all four.

🚨 **The root cause is an instrument, not the lint.** CI's ratchet job is two lines — generate the
eslint JSON, then compare it. **`npm run lint:ratchet` was only the second line.** It reads whatever
sits at a fixed `/tmp` path, so all day it read a report written before any of today's work existed
and printed `2889 files, 717 violations (baseline 717)`. CI regenerated and saw `2893 files, 719`.
⭐ **A green local instrument and a red CI job out of the same script.**

⚠ **The denominator was in the output the entire time — 2889 vs 2893 — and I read past it.** When
two runs of "the same" check disagree, the file count is where it shows first.

⭐ **The lesson that generalises: "did the instrument produce output?" and "did the instrument
measure THIS?" are different questions.** The script already refused a MISSING, unparseable or EMPTY
report — three carefully-handled ways of measuring nothing, each with its own exit code. It had no
defence against a report that is complete, parseable, and describes a *different tree*. I was
applying exactly that rule to DB A/Bs the same afternoon ("a reading taken while its subject changed
is not a reading") and never turned it on my own toolchain.

**Any check that consumes an artifact it did not produce can be handed a stale one.** The shape to
look for: a fixed path, a `--report`/`--input` flag, a build output compared to a baseline.

Fixed three ways: the npm script now generates the report itself (this is the part that removes the
class); `findStaleness()` refuses a report older than any file it claims to have linted, and names
the worst offender; and a test pins that CI's own job still generates before comparing, because a
guard's blast radius is fixed by what runs it.

⚠ **Also corrected a false claim in that guard's test header** — *"This repo does not run eslint in
CI and ci.yml says so."* Raw eslint is not a gate; the **ratchet job is blocking and runs eslint**.
That sentence is part of why I trusted the local number. **A comment that mis-describes what runs a
guard is a defect in the guard.**

⚠ Process note for the next session: I ran `npm test`, `tsc` and the ratchet before every push and
never once opened CI. **Local gates green is not CI green** — and this is the second time this
session that shape has cost something (the first was the docs-only fast path that never existed).
Check the run, not the local echo of it.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the cap sweep is closed: 19 candidates, 4 real, and the HIT RATE is the finding

Last confirmation and the close-out of #57, opened this morning.

**`/api/support-report`** computed `totalMessages`, `uniqueSessions`, per-category counts,
`dailyVolume` and **`deflectionRate`** from one unbounded `select("*")`. Live: **666 conversations in
the default 7-day window**, and `days` was **unclamped**, so `?days=11` already crossed the cap.
⭐ **A rate over a truncated denominator is wrong in an unpredictable DIRECTION, not merely low** —
1,000 deflected + 1,000 escalated is 50%, and truncated it reads **100%**, a plausible number and
therefore the dangerous kind. Same three lines also had `parseInt("abc")` → NaN →
`new Date(NaN).toISOString()` **throwing a RangeError**; both sibling routes already guard it with a
comment saying exactly this. **A fix applied per-file rather than per-pattern leaves siblings
behind** — the same lesson as "grep for the expression, not the file", one layer up.

⭐ **THE HIT RATE IS THE REAL RESULT: 19 candidates, 4 defects, 15 not — and 14 of the 15 for the
identical reason.** The bound is real but lives in a *different statement* from the `.from()`, so a
single-chain regex window ends before it. **A static scan of query chains has roughly a 20% precision
ceiling on this codebase.** That is precisely why I filed the sweep as candidates and refused to turn
the 197-site superset into a ban-at-zero guard this morning: it would have been ~90% false alarms and
suppressed within a week. Filing a scan's output as findings would have burned a day and a guard.

⚠ **The 15th refutation is the one I'd have got wrong at a glance.** `lib/studio-sales-history.ts`
reads `sales` by `edition_id` and **367 editions have over 1,000 sales** (max 20,339) — so it IS
truncated. But that set only pre-filters duplicates ahead of an insert whose `23505` path falls back
to per-row inserts: **the unique constraint is the real guard and the read is an optimisation.** A
performance cost, not a wrong answer, and deliberately not fixed because it writes to `sales`.
**"Is it truncated?" and "does the truncation change an answer?" are two questions.**

⚠ **Two mutants survived again and both were worth chasing.** The no-progress cursor guard (second
time today — no fixture produces "the same full page forever" unless you write one), and the
in-memory re-sort, which `dailyVolume` and `topCategories` cannot see because they sort themselves.
⭐ **When a mutant survives, ask which OUTPUT could observe it** — here the answer was "one of six",
and `escalatedDetails` now pins it.

Left alone with the reason recorded: `ufc-enrichment-drain` and `ufc-studio-sales-history-backfill`
read all UFC editions unbounded, and there are 518. Correct today, one collection-expansion from
being this morning's badge bug.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · an AVERAGE doing a bound's job, and an unordered LIMIT that churned

Two more confirmed out of #57, and between them they cover both ways this silent-truncation class
gets justified in a comment.

**`compute-laliga-pack-ev`** read the Golazos `pack_drop_pool` unbounded **and unordered**. The pool
holds **1,958 rows across 211 dist_ids**; the first 1,000 in physical order carry **134 of 211**, so
77 distributions could not get an EV row on any tick. ⭐ **And which 77 changed between runs** —
physical order shifts as the table is rewritten, so `pack_ev_history` has eventually seen all 211
while any single run covers a fraction. That churn is the tell I would have missed if I had only
compared the two counts. ⚠ **I nearly over-claimed here:** the latest run wrote 39 dists, far under
the 134 the cap allows, so the EV RPC's own `ok !== true` filter drops most of the rest. The cap is
one of two causes and the fix removes the non-deterministic one; the ledger says so rather than
banking the whole gap.

**`ownership-onchain-walk`** justified its unbounded read with *"avg ~23/wallet, well under
PostgREST's 1000-row cap"*. ⭐ **An average says nothing about the tail, and the tail is exactly who a
verification walk exists for.** Measured: 270,424 rows over 7,903 owners (avg 34), **max 26,737, and
21 owners over 1,000**. For those the walk confirmed a fraction of their holdings and reported the
rest as not-confirmed — on the pipeline whose only job is confirming ownership. This is the third
comment today whose factual claim had expired (after "a small table (hundreds of rows)" and "dormant
since the shutdown"), and the first where the number was never a bound in the first place.

⛔ Three more refuted: `market/route.ts:248`, `analytics/insider/signals:39`, `edition-history:69`.
⚠ **All five refutations so far are multi-statement builders** — `let q = …; if (x) q = q.eq(…);
await q.limit(50)` — so that is a limitation of the scan, not a coincidence, and it is recorded in
#57. **8 of 19 triaged in total: 3 confirmed and fixed, 5 refuted, 11 left.**

⚠ **A mutant survived and was worth the extra test.** The no-progress cursor guard (stop when the
next cursor equals the current one) killed nothing, because no fixture produced the pathological
"same full page forever" case. Added a termination test: without the guard the loop runs to its page
ceiling and the confirmed count becomes nonsense rather than merely incomplete. **A safety belt with
no test is indistinguishable from dead code.**

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the Pinnacle sniper couldn't price 30% of its editions, and a miss DROPS the listing

First item triaged out of the #57 list I'd just filed, and the worst landing of the three found today.

`lib/sniper/pinnacle.ts`'s `loadFmvMap()` read `pinnacle_catalog` unbounded. **2,470 rows carry an
`fmv_usd`; the first 1,000 under the old ordering cover 290 of the 416 distinct legacy edition keys —
69.7%.** And `flowtyNftToSniperDeals` does `if (!fmvData || fmvData.fmv <= 0) return []`: **a map miss
DROPS the listing.** So ~30% of Pinnacle editions could not appear on the sniper board however they
were priced, and the board read as honestly quiet because nothing errored.

⚠ **The module's own comment says the Flowty leg is "dormant since the 2026-05-13 shutdown".** It
isn't — production returned `flowtyCount: 42` with real deals while I was measuring. Second time
today that a comment's factual claim had expired: **a comment's claim about LIVENESS is a dated
sample, exactly like a row count.** I nearly wrote this off as a dead path on the strength of it.

⭐ **The fix forced a design change I would not have made on my own.** Keyset paging alone would have
been wrong: the representative render per key was picked by a global `ORDER BY fmv_sales_count_30d
DESC, fmv_usd DESC` plus first-wins, which needs the read to be COMPLETE *and* TOTALLY ORDERED.
Neither held — there was no unique tiebreak, so two identical requests could disagree about which
render represents a key, and under paging first-wins over a page order that is not the ranking order
is simply wrong. **Paging a read whose semantics depend on its ORDER means moving that decision out
of the ORDER BY and into an explicit comparison.**

⭐ **Two of the 19 candidates were REFUTED, which is why they were filed as candidates.**
`lib/concierge/fmv-distribution.ts:202` and `app/api/support-chat/route.ts:2172` both carry a
deliberate `.limit(500)` / `.limit(50)`; my scanner's chain window ends before it because those
queries are built across several statements (`let q = …; if (x) q = q.eq(…); await q.limit(50)`).
**A multi-statement builder defeats a single-chain regex** — recorded in #57, and the reason every
remaining candidate has to be read rather than counted. 3 of 19 done: 1 confirmed, 2 refuted.

Seven behavioural cases; the mock records the cursors the loop passed, so "it paged" is asserted as
*the cursor advanced to page 1's last render_id* rather than "more than one read happened".
Mutation-tested 6/6 (single-page, no-short-break, first-wins, last-wins, no-tiebreak,
drop-all-on-error).

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the sniper's badge map saw 10.6% of the table, and the COMMENT was why

Triaging the sniper-feed timeouts from the entry above turned up a second, quieter defect in the same
route: two reads had silently outgrown PostgREST's 1,000-row cap.

| read | rows (measured today) | returned | coverage |
|---|---|---|---|
| `badge_editions`, Top Shot scope | **9,471** | 1,000 | **10.6%** |
| `players`, `nba_top_shot` with a jersey | **1,317** | 1,000 | 76% |

⭐ **The comment was the defect.** The badge read carried an explicit justification — *"Safe because
badge_editions is a small table (hundreds of rows)"* — plus a measured *"4,981 rows / 4,981
distinct"*. Both were true when written. **A row count in a comment is a dated sample, never a
bound**, and a reader who checks the justification finds a number and stops looking. This repo
already applies that rule to its own memory files and had not applied it to code.

⛔ **The worse consequence is a control claiming a filter it applied to a truncated map.** With the
cap in place, ticking **Badges only** hid ~89% of the badged listings that exist and the board read
as nearly empty — *honestly* empty, because nothing errored. The degraded-source signal shipped
hours earlier cannot see this: it is the third state (read ok + truncated), not the first.

⚠ **The existing cap guard could not see it, and the reason generalises.**
`invariants-postgrest-cap.test.ts` is scoped to ONE idiom — a raw `fmv_snapshots` read ordered
`computed_at DESC`. It is structurally silent about every other unbounded read, **including two in a
file its own allowlist already names.** Being listed in a guard's allowlist is not coverage.

Fixed by keyset paging (`external_id` unique within the collection, so `.gt(cursor).limit(1000)` is
deterministic and O(n)): measured 1,935 buffers / 118 ms on the last page vs 7,698 / 187 ms for the
equivalent `.range()` page, because an OFFSET page re-walks everything before it.

**A sweep came out of it and is filed rather than shipped** (known-issues #57): 339 `.select()` sites
→ 197 on tables over 1,000 rows → **19 with no row-bounding filter at all**. ⚠ Deliberately NOT
turned into a ban-at-zero guard: most of the 197 are legitimately `.in()`-chunked, and a guard that
cries wolf gets suppressed. Equally deliberately NOT called 19 findings — a static heuristic is a
candidate list, and each needs its own live count under its own filter first.

⚠ **Two testing traps, both worth remembering.** (1) A `/\.range\(/` assertion **survived** a mutant
that deleted the call — the source *comment* beside it says ".range()" too. Fixed by pinning the
paging EXPRESSION, which prose does not carry, rather than reaching for the comment stripper this
repo has been blinded by three times. (2) `makeSupabaseFixture` was built ONCE in the badge suite's
mock, and a SEQUENCE fixture's cursor lives inside that instance and is not reset by clearing
`fx.tables` — so the third test to use a sequence starts mid-array and reds correct code. Rebuilt per
`seed()`.

⚠ **And a scripted replace silently matched nothing** while I was mutation-testing (my pattern said
`// listing itself`, the source says `// the listing itself`), so a mutant reported as SURVIVED had
never been applied. Asserting the occurrence count is what caught it — this file already carries that
rule.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the Pack Sniper told four users to widen their filters while All Day 403'd it

Same defect class as the boards above, one layer lower — and the first instance in this file found by
a **victim count** rather than an audit. `get_runtime_errors` over 24h:
`[sniper-feed] AD GQL FAILED: HTTP 403 <title>block</title>` — **4 occurrences, 4 users**. That route
is on-demand and user-facing, not a pipeline (`pipeline_runs_daily` returns `[]` for `%sniper%`), so
those are four collectors who got a wrong answer on the surface that tells them what is underpriced.

`fetchAlldayGqlPage` returned an empty page from a 403, from a GQL `errors[]` payload, from a missing
`searchMarketplaceEditions`, and from a thrown fetch. The route answered **200 with `deals: []`** and
the board concluded *"THE FLOOR IS QUIET — No deals match your filters. Try widening your search."*

⭐ **The finding I did not expect: the BUTTON is part of the claim.** Underneath that copy sat
**CLEAR FILTERS**, which asserts the same false diagnosis in the one place a frustrated reader is
most likely to click — and in a form that cannot be read as hedging. Fixing the sentence and leaving
the call to action would have half-fixed it. It now offers RETRY.

Nine deal-bearing reads collapsed the same way in this one route. Two of them are only deal-bearing
on a second look: a failed `get_editions_for_sniper` costs the edition key, and without a key a
listing has no FMV to price against, so it is dropped downstream; a failed All Day `fmv_current` map
