empties the board because a listing with no FMV is EXCLUDED rather than priced off its own ask.
Enrichment reads (badges, jersey numbers, pack EV) are deliberately NOT noted — noting them would
fire the degraded copy on a board that is honestly empty.

⚠ **The receiver was an allowlist, so the instrumentation half nearly shipped dead.**
`/api/public/log/empty-sniper` builds its logged payload by enumerating fields. Adding `degraded` /
`sourcesFailed` to the client beacon alone would have recorded **nothing**, with no error anywhere,
while reading as instrumentation in the diff AND in the client test. Caught by opening the receiver;
now pinned as a round trip. Generalised into the honesty doc: adding a field to any beacon means
opening the thing that logs it.

⚠ **Mutation testing found the two branches my fixtures could not reach.** 11 `sink.note` sites; the
first sweep killed 9. Both survivors were `catch` arms — supabase-js RETURNS errors rather than
throwing, so a suite that only sets `{ error }` never enters them. Two fixtures added, 11/11 killed.
Three client mutants each red 2 cases.

Also fixed a cache amplifier the 25 s TTL made easy to wave off: without evicting a degraded build,
one reader's 403 is served to every reader of the same filter combination for the rest of the window
— the ISR-caches-a-failed-read shape at lambda scale.

Gates: 1,426 files / 15,777 tests green (+2 files, +40 tests), `tsc` clean, eslint ratchet 717 =
baseline over 2,889 files.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · four more boards concluded from a failed read, and three blamed the reader's filters

Ran the honesty canon's own fifth-layer predicate — *"server pages that KNOW `ok` and seed a client
component without passing it"* — which the 2026-08-24 sweep wrote down, applied to five boards, and
stopped. **10 candidates, 4 real, 6 cleared.**

⭐ **Three of the four blame the FILTERS**, which is a worse sub-class than "there are none": "No
sales match those filters" sends the reader to widen filters that were never the problem. That is the
canon's actionable-harm test aimed at the reader's query instead of their account.

⚠ **My first pass used the wrong predicate and would have missed all four.** I checked whether the
FILE carried any provenance signal — and every one of these renders `<DegradedDataNotice>` from the
very `ok` it then drops, so all four passed. The 08-24 write-up says explicitly that a banner is not
a fix and that the predicate is about the PAGE→CLIENT handoff. I had read that section only after
running my own sweep.

⚠ **`parallel-premiums` is the instructive one: it already had the right sentence.** Its client
renders "Couldn't load these filters just now — this says nothing about which parallels match." for
its own refetch failure, and initialised that state to `false` unconditionally. The concept was
present; only the seed was missing. Any grep for "does this board handle a failed read?" answers yes.

Swept two sibling layers clean in the same pass and recorded the method, because a negative result is
only reusable if you can see what it covered: layer 1 (37 public API routes) and layer 4 (29 insights
OG cards). Both fine — the routes without the helper are media proxies and deliberate hand-rolled
error bodies; the OG cards without it fall back to product copy or `—`, never a claim.

All six new cases are SSR-asserted and mutation-tested; each failure case is paired with a no-change
control so that deleting an empty state cannot pass it.

🚨 **Then verifying the deploy found a panel the diff review had not.** I fetched the live SSR HTML
to confirm the healthy path still rendered (132 `<tr>` on cross-collection, zero degraded copy) — and
in doing so noticed `rookie-board` has a **burn view** over the same `initialRows`, still saying "No
burned rookie editions match those filters." ⭐ **"Fix per PANEL, not per page" applies inside one
client, not only across pages** — and the thing that caught it was checking the deployed output,
which is the step that is easiest to skip once the tests are green.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the cron fleet is clean, and the one watcher that cannot go green

Swept every post-boundary pg_cron failure: **6 in 67 hours, all explained, none live** — jobid 87
(fixed today), jobid 434 (mine, fixed today), one timeout at the boundary itself, one in-flight row my
`status <> 'succeeded'` filter miscounted, one `job canceled` on a job since unscheduled, and one
`invalid transaction termination` on jobid 430 that another session already diagnosed and replaced
with jobid 432 (succeeding, 52 s, `CALL`). Also checked for INVALID or leftover `*_ccnew` indexes:
**zero**.

**What the sweep did surface is a rate.** `wmc-reindex-verify` is red, and the reason is real:
`idx_wmc_cohort_cover` went 165.7 MB / 81.14% density (post-reindex, 08-31) → 299.2 MB / 45.13%
(09-01) → 326.4 MB (09-02). **~64 MB/day, so a REINDEX buys about a week**, and its pre-campaign size
was 614.4 MB. That turns "should the reindex be scheduled?" from a judgement into a number.

🚨 **And the watcher cannot clear itself.** `wmc-reindex-verify` has run 3 times ever, fails while any
target is under 60% density, and **no reindex job is scheduled** — a permanently-red instrument, which
is the shape this repo warns is indistinguishable from a broken one. Filed as known-issues #56.

⛔ **I did not book the job**, and the reason is coordination rather than technical: the campaign
belongs to another session's thread that deliberately did not re-book it, and a concurrent session was
pushing to `main` throughout this measurement (its concierge commit landed mid-rebase). What is needed
is a cadence decision, not a diagnosis.

⚠ Two care-points recorded with it: density was **not** re-probed today because `pgstatindex` reads the
whole index (~900 MB across the three targets), so the 45.13% is labelled as 09-01's reading rather
than passed off as current; and a pg_cron `failed` on a REINDEX job does **not** mean the work did not
happen, because `REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY` commits its phases.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · a sixth control for the fleet finding, and one lead that looked dead and was not

**The corroboration.** The `wallet-backfill*` pipelines are HTTP routes logging to `pipeline_runs`,
not pg_cron jobs — a different instrument over a different population, so nothing in the
`cron.job_run_details` measurement can leak into them. Their failures collapse on the same boundary
with DB-contention errors: `wallet-backfill-pinnacle` **225/1,125 → 0/375 → 0/571**,
`wallet-backfill-ufc` 25/494 → 0/375 → 0/571. ⚠ And those failures were **losing rows** —
`wmc_upsert_chunk_failures=N rows_lost=200…800` on most pre-boundary days, none after. The drain
bought `wallet_moments_cache` completeness, not just time.

⛔ **The lead I nearly filed as a defect.** `wallet-backfill-golazos` writes `rows_written = 0` on 30
of the last 35 days, and Golazos is the only live collection whose `wallet_moments_cache` rows have
not been re-stamped in three days — while every other collection is minutes fresh. That reads exactly
like a pipeline that reports success and does nothing, which is this repo's most productive defect
class.

**It is not one.** `rows_found` is **8,000–25,000 per day** and `rows_skipped` ≈ `rows_found`: it
reads every Golazos moment every day and finds nothing changed. `rows_written` counts CHANGES, so 0
is the honest value for a quiet 133-wallet collection, and `last_seen_at` is only re-stamped on a
write. ⭐ **The discriminator was `rows_found`, one column over from the one that looked alarming** —
and it is the third of the three incompatible meanings CLAUDE.md already assigns to a zero there.
Recorded as a NOT-A-FINDING so the next sweep does not re-open it.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · re-read the one "blocked" DB fix, and its premise is a shape nothing calls

CLAUDE.md's prioritized-actions list carried one measured-but-unshipped DB fix —
`compute_pack_ev_per_edition_weighted`'s `fmv_current` leg at **18,766 vs 1,046,192 buffers**, held on
a fixture re-seed that is Trevor's call — with an explicit instruction attached: *"re-read a 'blocked'
item's blocker before inheriting it."*

Taking that literally: **the 1,046,192 figure measured a `LEFT JOIN fmv_current` over 3,097 edition ids
at once, and no caller does that.** All four call sites are per-dist. Measured on the exact 57-dist
atlas set jobid 217 drives: **1,746 ms / 152,542 buffers for the whole pass**, ~2,625 buffers per call.
Corroborated from the fleet side — jobid 217 completes a tick in ~2 s, which a 1M-buffer-per-call
function cannot do.

⭐ **The filed figure was never wrong. It measured a shape that was never run** — and neither the
ledger entry nor the CLAUDE.md pointer recorded which call site it came from. **A cost figure is only
actionable together with its call site.** The item is not closed (the fixture question is still
Trevor's, and the leg is still the more correct shape); what changed is the stakes, so nobody spends a
session unblocking a 55× win that is worth ~1.7 s.

The CLAUDE.md bullet was DISPLACED rather than extended — 400 → 396 chars, file at 39,972 of 40,000 —
with the full write-up in `roadmap-status.md`, which is what that file's header prescribes.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · the fleet quietly got 8× cheaper three days ago and three filed items are measuring history

I went looking for the next expensive cron job using a 14-day pooled ranking, and it pointed at
jobids 217, 71, 303 with tens of thousands of wasted seconds each. **All of it was history.**

⭐ **The committed triage query had already told me so and I stopped reading it.**
`supabase/analysis/cron-waste-triage.sql` classified every one of those as RECOVERED or UNPROVEN and
put only two jobs in LIVE — jobid 87 (which I then fixed) and my own jobid 434. Its whole design is to
prevent the pooled-ranking mistake, and I made the mistake anyway by going back to a hand-rolled
`sum(duration) WHERE failed` afterwards. **The instrument was right; I overrode it.**

**What is actually true.** `job startup timeout` stopped at **2026-08-30 18:26:00Z** and has not
recurred: **270 → 0** across matched 66.6 h windows, 52 jobs → 0, at 93% of the run volume. The cause
is that the work got cheaper, not that anything was re-staggered: successful-run database time went
**201,812 s → 25,682 s (7.9×)** at 96% of the run count, mean concurrency 1.02 → 0.11. Per job,
210 s → 15 s, 195 s → 2 s, 178 s → 1 s at unchanged run counts. That is the fleet-scale confirmation
of the 08-30/31 top-consumer drain, which had only been verified per query.

⚠ **The subsection in `cron-and-schedulers.md` re-derived EARLIER TODAY — "the rate is FLAT PER JOB …
unchanged" — is itself pooled across the change point.** Its reasoning about the mechanism is right
and I kept it verbatim; only its currency was wrong. Two sessions in one day walking into the same
pooled-window trap, in the file that documents that trap, is the thing worth remembering.

I ran four controls before believing an 8× improvement: the instrument still records failures (it
caught two of mine on 09-02), the job mix is unchanged (118 → 117), Postgres has not restarted in 81
days and the worker-pool config is untouched, and the Top Shot outage is refuted as the cause because
sales ingest continued across the boundary.

🚨 **And then I got the outcome control wrong, in the way this repo documents.** I read
`pipeline_runs` over the same 66.6 h matched windows and reported "3 completed runs before vs 66
after" — a 22× claim I had already written into two docs before checking it. `pipeline_runs` retains
~73 h and that window sits 66.6–133 h back, so the rows were PRUNED, not missing.
⭐ **The window that is correct for one instrument is silently wrong for another: `cron.job_run_details`
holds 55 days, `pipeline_runs` holds three.** `pipeline_runs_daily` is indefinite and gives the true
answer — completed runs/day 17–23 of 24 → 24/24, with 57.0 rows per completed run on both sides, so
the work did not shrink. Caught before pushing, by asking what else could produce a missing row.

## 2026-09-02 (cont.) · a daily job that failed 15% of days on a lookup that could never match

Found by sweeping `cron.job_run_details` — which retains ~55 days here (201k rows), far
longer than `pipeline_runs`' ~73 h — for jobs whose failures cluster at ONE round
duration. `rpc-refresh-challenge-costs` died at **exactly 120.0 s** on 8 of 52 runs.
⭐ **A job that fails at exactly the same number every time is at a ceiling, not flaky**,
and that is a cheap sweep worth repeating.

The ceiling was this cluster's DEFAULT `statement_timeout` (120 s) — what a
`postgres`-owned pg_cron job runs under. Known-issues #43 said those jobs "run at the
cluster default" without ever naming the number.

**99.7% of the cost was one COALESCE arm**: a correlated scalar subquery against
`pack_ev_latest`, a `DISTINCT ON` view. The predicate cannot push through the DISTINCT,
so the whole view is re-materialised **once per outer row** — 40,716 ms / 21,094,324
buffers for 31 challenges, with the view's own `pack_ask_state` subplan running 3,849,487
times. Hoisted into one temp-table build: 1,220 ms / 681,430 buffers; the function now
runs in **1.208 s** live.

⚠ **And the arm returns NULL for every row** — all 29 reward dists are in
`pack_distributions`, zero are in `pack_ev_history`. I ruled out the two obvious
alternative explanations before believing it: the view's filters (the base table has no
rows for them either) and an id-vocabulary mismatch (same numeric-string space, and the
history's max dist id is *higher* than the challenges'). ⛔ Deleted nothing anyway — the
cost was the 31 evaluations, not the arm.

🚨 **My first post-state control was wrong, and the migration caught me.** I asserted that
no `cached_reward_value` changed; 18 of 31 changed and it rolled back. The stored values
were computed by the last successful run a day earlier, and arms 2–4 read tables that
move daily — **a before/after comparison spanning a refresh window measures the data
moving, not the code.** The correct control was narrower and exact, and 33× cheaper: only
arm 1 changed, and the two forms differ only where a reward dist appears in the view, so
`overlap = 0` proves equivalence outright.

## 2026-09-02 · red `main` fixed, and the Top Shot on-chain re-key was moved off PostgREST

**1. `main` was red for five commits and I caused it.** `ce85c45c6` added a line of prose
to `tooling-gotchas.md` quoting a Postgres regex — `'[?&](key|token|secret)='` — whose
character class abuts an alternation group, so the line contains the byte sequence `](`
and `scripts/check-memory-doc-links.mjs` read it as a pointer to a file named
`key|token|secret`. Two jobs failed (the standalone CI step and the vitest case that runs
the guard live) on `ce85c45c6`, `b5b713b4e`, `a744f11a4`, `e794ecf9c`, `180f9ee7f`.
Everything else was green throughout. The guard now blanks fenced blocks and inline code
spans; three fixtures pin it, including the positive control that a dead link OUTSIDE the
backticks on the same line still fails. Mutation-tested by reverting the strip.

⚠ **The lesson is the SWEEP, not the regex.** Every one of those five commits was
docs-only, and I ran the register-integrity, inbox-index and ledger guards after each
edit. I never ran the link guard, because "I only touched docs" reads as *below* the CI
surface — when it is exactly the surface a docs-only commit is gated by. **There is no
docs-only fast path.**

**2. `topshot-misattrib-drain`'s re-key was being rolled back on half its runs.** Found by
asking why 10 Top Shot pipelines read 100% failed over 72 h; nine of those are the known
upstream 530 outage (already filed, known-issues #50), but this one's error string was
different: `rekey: upstream request timeout`, a **gateway** timeout, on a WRITE RPC.

⭐ **The control settles what the error string cannot.** Day-by-day, `pipeline_runs_daily`
against the function's own audit table: on all five `upstream request timeout` days
(08-23 → 08-28) `audit_topshot_sale_drain_remap_20260621` gained **zero** rows, against
673 on the one clean day. **The gateway giving up is a ROLLBACK.** The existing docs said
the statement "keeps running server-side", which is true about cost and silent about
commit — and the two compound: full IO cost, no rows.

⚠ **Why it hid.** The re-key is idempotent, so the next successful run catches up, no
backlog grows, and no downstream metric moves. Re-derived live: 0 sales / 10 moments
currently need a re-key, i.e. the work is current. This was waste plus a lying `ok=false`,
not data loss — which is exactly why nothing found it.

⛔ **"Make the query faster" was measured and rejected.** The sales leg scans 3,198,302
Top Shot rows (174,820 buffers / 9.0 s warm) and the planner's hash join is the RIGHT
plan: forcing the nested loop over the per-partition `nft_id` indexes costs 1,315,991
buffers / 33.3 s. Shipped instead: pg_cron **jobid 434 `rpc-topshot-onchain-rekey`**
under `cron_heavy`, whose role `statement_timeout` is 600 s, with `?rekey=1` dropped from
`vercel.json`.

**3. 🚨 And the fix broke on its own first tick, which is the most useful thing that
happened all session.** Jobid 434 fired at 11:33 UTC and died in **0.0 s**:
`permission denied for function run_topshot_onchain_rekey`. The mandated anon hardening
(`REVOKE EXECUTE … FROM PUBLIC, anon, authenticated`) removes the PUBLIC grant that is
`cron_heavy`'s ONLY path to execute a new public function — `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` on
this database covers anon/authenticated/service_role and not cron_heavy. **Two standing
policies in direct conflict — and I walked into a trap this repo had already documented three
times** (`database.md`, 2026-08-23: two Pinnacle trade jobs and a series rollup). My migration's
header says nothing said so; that is wrong, and the correction is recorded in `database.md`,
`cron-and-schedulers.md` and the ledger. ⭐ **Three prose records did not prevent a fourth
instance, because prose is read after a session knows its topic and this trap fires while you
are thinking about anon safety. What was missing was a CHECK, not knowledge.**

⚠ **The failure shape is worse than the error.** 0.0 s, no `pipeline_runs` row (the
function never runs, so it never logs), message only in `cron.job_run_details` — which no
fleet sweep here reads. A job in that state is indistinguishable from one never
scheduled: silent, free, green everywhere. It was caught only because I watched the first
tick on purpose. Fixed with an explicit `GRANT … TO cron_heavy` (the convention: 48 of
the other 49 cron_heavy-called functions already carry `cron_heavy=X/postgres`), plus
`check_cron_heavy_job_exec_drift()` — population derived from `cron.job` by walk, returns
`{inspected, offenders}` so a run that inspected nothing cannot read as clean. Live:
inspected 56, offenders 0.

**4. Verified end-to-end, and one claim of mine was wrong for an hour.** The first
successful run took **75,803 ms** (11:35:48→11:37:04Z, ok, 0 sales / 0 moments re-keyed,
10 moments deferred on genuine collisions). **63% of the gateway's ~120 s, 13% of
`cron_heavy`'s 600 s** — which is the whole argument for the move in one number.

⚠ That run was issued through the Supabase **MCP** `execute_sql` tool, which gave up at
its own 60 s budget. I checked `pipeline_runs` at ~11:36, saw zero rows, and recorded it
as a second replication of the rollback finding. **It committed at 11:37:04.** I had taken
a reading while its subject was still changing — the exact trap this repo's measurement
rules name. The gateway-504 rollback finding stands on its own control (the audit table,
5 for 5); the MCP tool timeout is a DIFFERENT layer and does **not** abort the statement.
Both are now written down, separately, so nobody carries one across to the other.

## 2026-09-02 (early hours) — a failed READ that lands on a retirement, and four register rows that were no longer true (Claude Code, cloud session, push-enabled)

Continuation of the 09-01 night pass. Two threads: one new defect **class**, and a batch of filed
findings re-derived rather than inherited. Durable lessons are promoted to
`docs/reference/key-files-and-honesty.md`, the register and the ledger — not left here.

### The class: `const { data } = await …` where `not found` is RECORDED, not re-tried

The shape appears **25 times** across `app/`+`lib/`+`workers/`. **Most are harmless**, so a ban is the
wrong instrument. 👉 **The discriminator is not the expression, it is WHERE `not found` LANDS.** In an
enrichment loop a miss costs one un-enriched row and the next tick fixes it. In these seven routes it
landed on:

- **`retry_count + 1`**, and ten bumps RETIRE the row permanently — at `*/15` a sustained read failure
  would have retired every queued row in **two and a half hours**, with no trace but a rising
  `rows_skipped`, which reads as the pipeline working;
- **a cursor that advanced anyway** (`topshot-offers-indexer`) — zero rows written, `ok: true`, and
  nothing revisits a block below the cursor, so the offers are **gone**;
- **a Cadence storm** (`allday-resolve-unmapped`) — `existing` empty ⇒ every edition "missing" ⇒ one
  on-chain call per id, then a mass upsert back over rows already there;
- **a flooded retry queue** (`allday-listings-indexer`), worked with real Cadence borrows and a finite
  budget, for rows that were never unresolvable;
- **`edition_id: null` forever** (`pinnacle-trades-indexer`) — `ignoreDuplicates: true` means no later
  tick corrects it.

**Two siblings found in the same files.** `allday-listings-retry` marked rows `resolved_at` whose v2
upsert had **failed** — the listing is then lost and nothing re-derives it. And all three retry routes
ended their resolved-mark with a log-only error branch followed by `resolved = list.length`; the admin
route answered its operator `{ok: true, resolved: true}`, the one signal that stops a human looking.

⭐ **And the exception is now written down.** `wallet-backfill`'s `seeded_wallets` read discards its
error too, and SHOULD: a NaN there sends the wallet down the full-walk path — more work, never a wrong
answer. It carries a comment saying so. **A rule that cannot name its own exceptions gets applied
mechanically until someone breaks something with it.**

Fifteen tests, every one pinning an **absence** (the retry row at `retry_count: 9` receives zero
UPDATEs; the cursor write does not happen; 87% never appears) and every one mutation-checked.

### Also shipped

- **`/api/collection-stats` ran the same 19,942-probe FMV scan twice per request** — the route
  recomputed, through `query_sql`, a scan `get_collection_stats` had already made. **116,945 buffers /
  2,875 ms / 19,942 lateral loops** off every uncached request. Folded, with **equivalence proved for
  all five collections in a SINGLE STATEMENT** so a concurrent write cannot fake a mismatch.
  🚨 **And a 291× false claim found on the way:** the "Priced from Sales" KPI fell back to `fmv_pct`, a
  different metric — **Golazos 87.3% against a true 0.3%**, UFC 73.6% against 0.0%.
- **deep-audit D39 closed.** The alert path could not see its own cache going stale. ⛔ **The reader was
  the wrong place to fix it:** its consumer builds `detail` by concatenating `->>` reads, so a
  differently-shaped "stale" marker would have emitted an alert with a **NULL detail**. The staleness
  went on the CALLER. Threshold measured (30 d: max gap 422 min vs the arm's 24 h), and the post-state
  proves the arm is **silent** on a fresh cache AND that it **can** fire.
- **`allday-price-recover` watchlisted** at a threshold from 228 measured gaps, not the nominal cadence.
- **Top Shot `wallet-backfill` had no denominator** — every sibling lane emits `rows_to_write`; the
  biggest one did not, and its three non-happy exit paths emitted no chunk fields at all, so **the run
  most likely to have lost rows was the one that hid it.**

- **The null-serial sale backfill was reading two sources and a third had the answer.**
  `backfill_null_serial_sales_from_moments` (hourly) resolved a missing `sales.serial_number` from
  `moments` then `wallet_moments_cache`. Adding `nft_edition_map` as a **strictly third** leg and
  running one unbounded pass wrote **2,307 serials**: AllDay's null-serial population with a real
  `nft_id` went **2,295 → 0**, UFC **10 → 0**, Golazos **2 → 0**. Top Shot's 1,094 stay — they belong to
  the separately-dead edge lane, and `nft_edition_map` gained **2** of them.
  ⭐ **Two independent gaps lived in one function and I nearly shipped the wrong one.** My first sizing
  said "824 AllDay rows are one COALESCE away"; measuring *inside* the 45-day window found 1,096
  candidates, **all Top Shot**, of which the new leg gained **2**. The AllDay rows were all OLDER than 45
  days — the **age window**, not the missing leg, was what excluded them. Both were real; the drain pass
  closes the age one for the backlog and the leg keeps it closed inside 45 days.
  ⭐ **And it retired a work item without doing that work item.** The 08-55Z filing had proposed an
  escalating cooldown for `nfl_all_day / onchain_nil / not_in` — **700 rows, 9,959 attempts since
  08-07** — gated on measuring how many were permanently unrecoverable. That measurement is moot:
  `get_serial_backfill_targets` keys on `serial_number IS NULL OR = 0`, so all **2,144** AllDay failure
  rows now point at resolved sales and the picker returns **0** AllDay targets. 👉 **Before tuning how
  often you re-ask a failing upstream, check whether anything local already knows the answer — a retry
  policy fixes a *rate*, never a *gap*.**
  Pinned with 4 new assertions (the new leg wins alone, loses to `moments`, loses to `wmc`, and its
  `> 0` + collection scoping both bind), each mutation-checked; the drift guard was repointed in the
  same commit.

- **`resolve-topshot-stubs`: 37 rows written in 74,800 chain lookups over 36 days, and our own
  database had the answer for two-thirds of them.** `wallet_moments_cache` carries a `player_name`
  for 346 of the 515 nameless Top Shot stub editions — and those stubs were created *from* wmc rows
  by `stub_editions_from_wmc`, which writes `player_name` as `NULL, -- player to be resolved later`
  while reading a row that has it. 267 filled; the stub queue went **520 → 253** and the resolver's
  ~2,300 daily chain lookups roughly halve, so the change **displaces** work instead of adding it.
  🚨 **The control did not come back clean, and that is the interesting part.** Against editions that
  already carry a name, 46 of 11,866 disagree — mostly Steph/Stephen — but three name a **different
  person** (Trae Young recorded as Alex Sarr, John Collins as Matas Buzelis, Julius Randle as Andre
  Drummond), and all three sit on edition_keys where wmc holds more than one distinct name. Split on
  that, over the subedition keys where the risk lives: **n=1 → 2,750 checked, 0 wrong players; n>1 →
  17 checked, 10 wrong (58.8%)**. ⭐ **A source that is 99.6% right overall and 58.8% wrong about
  *which person* on an identifiable subset is not a 99.6% source — it is two sources, and finding the
  discriminator is what makes it usable.** The 79 ambiguous stubs stay nameless on purpose: a Dynamic
  Duos play really has two players.
  ⚠ **Two of my own vacuous assertions, both caught and both recorded rather than quietly fixed.**
  The first migration's "positive control the change cannot move" was
  `player_name IS NOT NULL AND (player_name IS NULL OR = '')` — a contradiction, zero in every
  database in every state; a second migration supersedes it with a row-by-row check against what was
  actually written, recorded *before* the run so it cannot merely re-derive the function's own
  predicate. The second was in the pinned test: it claimed the UPDATE's never-clobber re-check was
  covered, and deleting that re-check leaves the file green (both halves read one snapshot; only a
  concurrent writer separates them). Fixed in the COMMENT, not by weakening the assertion. 6 of 7
  mutations red, the 7th named as uncovered.

- **Five backward history backfills could rewind their own walk on one failed read.** The
  `*-sales-history-backfill` family holds the LOWEST block already scanned in `event_cursor`, and
  every one of them discarded the error on that read — so a blip left `ceiling` at `CEILING_INIT`,
  the TOP of the walk, and the tick then **wrote that high block back over the real cursor**. Nothing
  re-walks a range above a cursor. A sibling defect in the same block assigned `cursorWritten`
  without checking the upsert, so a **failed advance logged as a movement** — and `p_cursor_after` is
  the only instrument anyone would notice this on. Fourteen chunked id lookups in the same files read
  as "unmapped" on failure and sent whole batches into the Cadence fallback. Ratchet **47 → 28**.
  ⚠ **No evidence it fired, and the caveat is the point:** `pipeline_runs` retains ~73 h, and over
  that window no run shows `cursor_after > cursor_before` nor a `cursor_before` above the previous
  run's `cursor_after`. **A clean 73 h is not a clean history.**
  ⭐ **And two apparently-dead walkers turned out to be correctly retired.** `ufc-` (stopped 08-27,
  10.25 M blocks short of the floor) and `topshot-flowty-` (stopped 08-17, exactly at it) both look
  like silent deaths; both carry `is_active = false` in the cadence watchlist with the reason — UFC
  migrated to Aptos in May, flowty finished. **Absence of runs is not a defect when the instrument
  that would alert on it was deliberately switched off and says why.**

- **Twenty-one indexers logged a cursor advance they had not made.** `cursorAfter` was assigned
  straight after an `await …from("event_cursor").update(…)` whose result was thrown away, so a write
  supabase-js refused still logged the new block and the next tick re-scanned the same range while
  the run log showed progress. ⭐ **The read ratchet could never have found these:** it matches
  `const { … } = await supabaseAdmin`, and a bare `await …update(…)` has nothing destructured to
  inspect. **Two guards, disjoint syntax — neither finds the other's population.** The new one is a
  **ban at zero** (cursor writes are few, uniform and consequential by construction), with a detector
  positive control on synthetic snippets and a behavioural test on `topshot-offers-indexer`.
  ⛔ **Whether it ever fired is NOT established, and the tempting evidence is a false positive.** The
  signature (run N claims `cursor_after = X`, run N+1 opens below X) shows `pinnacle-trades-indexer`
  at **436 of 874 runs**, which is innocent: that route runs two modes under one pipeline name against
  two cursors, one ascending and one descending. ⭐ **A cursor-continuity check that ignores WHICH
  cursor a run used reads a dual-mode indexer as 50% broken.**

- **The error-discarding-read population reached ZERO — and the ratchet went red for succeeding.**
  61 → 51 → 47 → 28 → 0 across the sales indexers, the five backward history walkers, ten forward
  indexers, `topshot-flowty-unmapped-drain` (where a failed local read routes an nft into an on-chain
  `getMinted` whose not-found **retires the row permanently**), `candy-sales-indexer` and two admin
  routes.
  🚨 **The last fix broke the guard.** Its not-vacuous floor asserted `>= 20` occurrences still matched
  **in the tree** — a floor over VIOLATIONS FOUND, wearing the name of a detector control. ⭐ **The
  tell is what the floor counts: over "files walked" or "fixtures classified correctly" it survives
  success; over "violations found" it cannot, and the two read identically.** The rule it broke is
  already in this repo, and the file's own header cited it. Replaced with nine assertions against
  synthetic source — including `{ data: cursorRow, error: cursorErr }`, the exact false positive this
  detector once had — and the ratchet converted to a **ban at 0**.
  ⭐ **`candy-sales-indexer`'s four reads each landed differently and got four different decisions**,
  including one that deliberately does NOT throw: `unresolved_open: unresolvedOpen ?? 0` is the
  fabricated-number shape verbatim, publishing a **measured zero** for the park-queue backlog out of a
  failed count — but it runs after every write of the sweep, so failing there would discard real work
  to report a statistic. It reports `null` plus a discriminator instead.

- **The null-serial recovery job could never see the rows the history backfills produce.** Eighty
  minutes after the drain took AllDay to zero it was back at **499**, none inside the hourly job's
  45-day window, **100% resolvable**. Not a backlog — a structural leak: the `*-sales-history-backfill`
  walkers write HISTORICAL sales, so their `sold_at` is older than 45 days **by construction**, and
  jobid 76 calls the function with no argument. Default widened 45 → 3650 (the cron COMMAND is
  unreachable — jobid 76 is `cron_heavy`-owned and no session-reachable role may `cron.alter_job` it).
  ⭐ **A bounded window is a claim about where the work comes from.** This one was written for a
  FORWARD indexer's recent rows and became wrong the day a BACKWARD walker started writing into the
  same table. **When you add a producer, re-read every consumer's window.**
  ⚠ **And the cost comparison nearly lied to me:** a bare `count(*)` over the candidate subquery lets
  the planner DROP the COALESCE subplans and reads 745 buffers where the real shape reads 10,593.
  Caught on the plan, not on the number. Like-for-like the change is **+5,302 buffers / +1.8 s per
  hourly run** — and the 45-day version was not a saving anyway, since all 1,094 rows it scanned
  resolve from none of the three legs.

- **The 102,000-row AllDay unmapped backlog is a PRICE backlog, and my first measurement of it was
  wrong in a way that would have filed a defect that does not exist.** A 1-in-50 sample said **22.9%
  of the open rows have a resolvable edition** — which reads as a broken resolver, since
  `promote_unmapped_sales` runs constantly and its own comment records finding "~0.6 newly resolvable
  per call". ⭐ **The sample omitted the function's price guard.** Split on it: of the **priced** rows
  **0.2%** are resolvable — exactly matching the function's own figure — while of the **unpriced**
  rows **39.7%** are. The composition is 93,438 `onchain_dapper_v1` rows, 58,779 of them unpriced,
  because a V1 Dapper sale whose tx-decode budget ran out lands at `price_usd = 0` and
  `COALESCE(price_usd,0) > 0` parks it on purpose — promoting it would pollute FMV.
  👉 **The bottleneck is decode throughput, not the edition maps**, and the drain exists and works:
  `allday-price-recover` wrote 8,710 rows in 7 days and the backlog moved 104,908 → 101,902.
  ⭐ **The lesson is the one this session kept re-learning from the other side: reproduce the
  consumer's OWN predicate before concluding it is failing.** Three times tonight a population looked
  broken until it was measured through the filter its consumer actually applies — here the price
  guard, on the null-serial job the age window, and on Pinnacle's `edition_id` the fact that it is not
  the resolution criterion at all.

### The register's own probes, re-run — and two of them lie in opposite directions

Thirteen `VERIFIED-CLEAN` rows re-run. Twelve returned their expected value. The two that did not are
the interesting ones, and they fail in opposite ways.

- 🚨 **`No hardcoded credentials` PASSED while the invariant was broken.** It greps `rpc_pls_` — the
  exact gate-key prefix — over `origin/main`, and reports `0 real hits`. Meanwhile **all 14
  HTTP-dispatching pg_cron jobs send that key IN THE URL**, 13 active, **0 using a header**, and
  Supabase edge-function logs record full request URLs. ⭐ **A credential probe is bounded by its
  CORPUS, not its regex** — a cron command lives only in the database, so it was never in scope, and
  "0 real hits" reads as an estate-wide all-clear. Same shape as the `?token=` leak fixed on
  `/api/cron/sales-serial-backfill` hours earlier, at fourteen times the count, on the leg nobody
  looked at. ⛔ Not fixed: the edge half is deploy-gated, and relocation must precede rotation or the
  new value is re-logged on the next tick — while relocation alone closes nothing, because these
  values are already burned in public git history.
- ⓘ **`pg_cron has no disabled/orphan jobs` FAILED while everything was fine.** 104/103; the one
  inactive job is a ledger-recorded pause for an outage that is still live. The row now says so, since
  the register's own rule is that a failed probe earns a full investigation — and this one would have
  sent the next auditor down the same path.
- **`pg_cron non-timeout error class` also failed benignly**, and the reason is durable: four of the
  five offending rows sit on jobids that no longer exist. **`cron.job_run_details` outlives
  `cron.job`**, so a one-off scratch job someone created and dropped reads as a new error class
  forever. The probe needs a LEFT JOIN.

⭐ **R29 re-derived, and the new shape changes the advice.** 384 startup timeouts = 76.6% of pg_cron
failures, unchanged — but per job it is **flat at ~1.1–1.9%** regardless of schedule, owner or
workload. The absolute leaders rank by run count alone. That corroborates the `max_worker_processes=6`
pool mechanism independently and means **there is no worst job to fix**: the ranking is not a culprit
list.

### Re-derived, and four of them had moved

⚠ **A filed finding is a hypothesis; so is a filed number.**

- **R25** resolved — 95 of 96 dispatches now log. ⚠ **Measure it over 72 h, not 7 d:** `pipeline_runs`
  retains ~73 h and `cron.job_run_details` does not, so the 7-day version reads 12-of-28 and invents a
  57% logging gap. **Two instruments with different retentions cannot be differenced over a window
  longer than the shorter one.**
- **R53** — 437 → **149 min/week**; `impossible-parallel` p50 489 → 187 s, fails 34.6% → 10.7%.
  Recorded, **not attributed**.
- **R54 / R55** closed on four clean ticks each. R55 does better than reading green: one run carries
  `truncated_steps: ["seed_recent"]` at 120,285 ms — the contract engaging, not just an absence of red.
- **R30's falsifier is durable now** (`extra_num_sums`, shipped hours earlier), and **08-30 already
  contradicts the zero**: AllDay 167,970 attempted / 15,425 lost, *after* the 08-28 experiment.
  ⚠ Do not divide those two keys naively — Pinnacle reads `lost > attempted`, so they are not over the
  same population.
- **pack-EV**: both measurements the 08-18 filing owed are done. **Zero editions carry snapshots under
  two collections** (so the feared pre-existing defect does not exist), and the **"cost crossover" is
  refuted** — the plan never flips from 3 to 3,097 ids. The `= ANY` route dies anyway, for a *measured*
  reason: its cost is **linear in the id count**, because it prunes which rows are read and not which
  **versions**. ⭐ **A refutation whose stated mechanism is false is not safe to inherit.**
- **wmc-metadata's exit condition, run 4 h early, answered the branch nobody expected**: `fillable`
  climbed 0 → 29, and the regeneration is on the **editions** side — an edition gaining a value flips a
  months-old wmc row with nothing touching `wallet_moments_cache` at all.

## 2026-09-01 (night) — six pipelines were green and doing nothing; found by one census, fixed, and two of my own fixes corrected within the hour (Claude Code, cloud session, push-enabled)

Started by draining the newest inbox filing and ended up with a class rather than a list. Every defect
below reported `ok`, and most reported a healthy row count while converting nothing. Durable lessons are
promoted to `docs/reference/key-files-and-honesty.md`, the register and the ledger — not left here.

**The instrument that found them.** A 30-day yield census over `pipeline_runs_daily` (indefinite,
unlike `pipeline_runs`' ~73 h). ⛔ **It is a triage list a human reads, never an arm** — of nine
pipelines it lists, seven are false positives, including the top row by a factor of twelve
(`snapshot-pack-asks`, whose `rows_found` is the size of the live listing set). **`rows_found` does not
mean the same thing twice across the fleet.**

**Shipped**

1. **`lock-check-batch`** — 9,590 checks/24 h to 12 SEEDED wallets, **0 to any saved or linked wallet**,
   at 48/48 ok. 1.47 M NULL `lock_checked_at` values make the ordering one huge tie, and mass decides.
   Added a wallet-class tier — in **two** places, because the priority leg's own `LIMIT` discards user
   rows before `ranked` sees them. Verified: 200/200 to user wallets.
   ⚠ A concurrent session shipped the same fix 3.5 min earlier under the same migration name; both
   applied rows are kept, because parity keys on NAME and one file would have hidden the other.
2. **`sales-counterparty-backfill`** — 288 runs/day, **10.25 h**, zero recovered. It had walked past
   Flow's prune horizon, which answers **HTTP 200** with `execution: "Pending"` and no events.
   24 `pg_net` probes bracket the wall inside 2023-11-08 to a window containing **no `sales` row at
   all**, so the floor partitions exactly. Verified: **0/120 → 109/120, 3× faster.**
3. **A race 42 s later** — an in-flight tick wrote its stale cursor back over the migration's reset, and
   the next tick returned 0 rows looking perfectly healthy. The claim now self-heals a cursor stranded
   below the floor.
4. **`rpc_ops_snapshot.pipeline_fails_24h`** — 51 of 82 failures were ONE Top Shot 530 across seven
   pipelines, so the list ranked a pipeline with fresh data above three that were losing rows. Now
   carries an `upstream` count and orders on `(fails - upstream)`, with a drift guard on the signature.
5. **`topshot-buyer-backfill-historical`** — re-decoded the **same 45 rows** 47×/day forever, with
   `decode_404`, `decode_failed` and `decode_other_status` all zero. Verified: 45 → 0 found, 12.0 s →
   5.2 s, and the 45 exhausted rows are now reported rather than silently absent.
6. **`pipeline_runs_daily.extra_num_sums`** — the daily rollup kept only which `extra` keys existed,
   never their values, so every per-step counter died at ~73 h. Three measurements this week ran into
   that, one of them fatally (R30's falsifier). Now durable — and it preserved tonight's own before/after.
7. **`allday-price-recover`** — a **9,859-row** recovery moving at ONE row per tick: no `ORDER BY`, so
   the same physical page every time, 999 of 1,000 discarded as multi-NFT. Its `CANDIDATE_LIMIT = 2000`
   was silently clamped to 1,000 by PostgREST, which is why `candidates` read exactly 1000 forever.
   Verified on the first tick: **`tx_decode_ok` 1 → 500, `skipped_multi_nft_rows` 999 → 0** — the
   backlog goes from ~137 days to about seven hours.
8. **`backfill-topshot-subedition-circulation`** — logged `ok: true` with `errors_sample: []` on every
   daily run while reading NOTHING, because `ok` came from per-edition WRITE errors and the failure was
   the upstream READ. With `ok` true it could not even trip the arm built for this shape, whose
   predicate is `ok_runs = 0`.
9. **`check_secdef_anon_execute_violations`** — the guard watching for anon-executable SECURITY DEFINER
   functions iterated **nine hardcoded names**; the schema has **559**. Found by asking Supabase's own
   advisor the same question and getting a different answer. Nothing is exposed — all four reachable
   rows are deliberate — but the zero it published meant almost nothing. Now a tree walk, 9 → 559.

**The two corrections, both of my own work, both within the hour**

- **"450,987 recoverable" was wrong by 10×.** It counted rows whose TRANSACTION IS REACHABLE, not rows
  whose SELLER IS DECODABLE — the same circular sizing the fmv-recalc filing described the day before,
  which I had read. It surfaced by watching the **second** tick: the first recovered 109/120 and the next
  six recovered **0 of 720**. Probed: 21 of 21 hash-bucket-sampled studio-history txs are
  `NFTStorefrontV2.ListingAvailable` with zero Withdraw events, against a 3-of-3 positive control. A
  listing moves no NFT. **Reachable 450,987 · decodable ≈ 38,200.**
- **The exclusion that fixed it had the NULL trap.** `NOT IN` yields NULL for a NULL source, so an
  unlabelled row would have been dropped silently — the shape of everything else fixed tonight,
  reintroduced by the fix for one of them. `IS DISTINCT FROM` attempts it instead.

**What I would tell the next session**

- ⭐ **Watch the SECOND tick.** The first tick after a fix is the one most likely to flatter it. Both
  corrections above came from the tick after the one I would have quoted.
- ⭐ **Reachable ≠ convertible.** A floor or a predicate tells you the first; only sampling the
  conversion tells you the second.
- ⭐ **Build a suppression list from the WALK'S OWN OUTPUT.** Mine came from another instrument's
  summary and the post-state caught the function I had skipped.
- ⭐ **A cursored backfill needs a FLOOR, not just a cursor** — a newest-first walk terminates only by
  running out of data.
- ⭐ **`res.ok` is not a liveness check** when the upstream answers 200 for data it cannot serve.

**Not shipped, deliberately.** `resolve-topshot-stubs` (37 rows in 74,800 attempts over 36 days) is
filed rather than fixed: its rotation works as designed and the cheap fix uses the same column for
"attempted" and "changed". The 408,309 undecodable and 37,832 unsplittable rows still inside their
predicates want re-classification, which is a large data mutation and deserves its own change.

**Verification still owed:** `backfill-topshot-subedition-circulation` runs daily at 21:10Z, so its
`ok:false` + `gql_fault_reason` land tomorrow, not tonight.

## 2026-09-01 (evening→night) — drained two cloud handoffs and both monitors, then found four defects by verifying my own work (Claude Code, Trevor's box, push-enabled)

Started as a drain of the 09-01 cloud handoffs and the two daytime monitors. Most of the value came
afterwards, from checking whether the things I had just shipped actually ran. Durable lessons are
promoted to CLAUDE.md, `docs/reference/*.md`, the ledger and memory — not left here.

**Shipped**
1. **20 fileless migrations recovered and committed** across the session (9 + 3 + 4 + 4), all byte-exact
   via `recover-fileless-migrations.mjs`, never retyped. Two sessions independently recovered the same
   nine and got byte-identical files — which is what makes the new CI auto-recovery safe to run
   unattended.
2. **`enrich-ufc-wallet`** reads FMV through `get_fmv_snapshot_for_editions` instead of 200-wide
   PostgREST slices. Confirmed live: **~185,457 → 3,268 buffers/call, ~29,400 → 97.5 ms**, and the old
   queryid left the top-12 of a 7.5 h `ops_pgss_delta`. Both halves of its EXIT met.
3. **`cache-refresh`** batches the `lock_checked_at` stamps (ten single-row UPDATEs → at most two).
4. **fmv-recalc Steps 5c/5d/5e** use a per-edition LATERAL instead of a whole-history `DISTINCT ON`.
5. **fmv-recalc historical fallback** made finishable, then its admission predicate corrected.
6. **`/insights/pack-reality`** stopped claiming the market is empty when our own prices are stale.
7. **`scripts/resolve-ledger-rebase-conflict.mjs`** + test — the rebase recipe as an executable.
8. **`backfill_wmc_metadata_from_editions` drained** (152 rows; `fillable` now 0).

**Closed by re-derivation, not by new work — #41 and #47.** Both recorded numbers that described regimes
which no longer existed. #41's `7 */6` schedule had been reverted; split at the change point, running the
job **3.2× MORE OFTEN** cut failures **23.1% → 4.0%** and waste 41% at unchanged total cost, because a
`REFRESH … CONCURRENTLY` costs what changed since last time. #47's falsifier is answered and not met
(6/12 → 0/5 kills), **but the duration collapse lands a day after the deploy**, so "the kills stopped" is
established and "the schedule change stopped them" is not — recorded as confounded rather than banked.

**The four defects, all found by verifying my own work rather than by looking for them**

1. **fmv-recalc's historical fallback had failed on 350 of 350 runs** and reported
   `historicalFallback=0` — indistinguishable from "nothing to do" on every instrument anyone reads.
   Found by asking whether the change I had *just* deployed actually executed, which needed the runtime
   logs because `pipeline_runs` cannot see it and pgss cannot see through `query_sql`.
2. **My own fix then failed its exit condition** — 23,800 editions "covered" for ~895 net progress. The
   admission predicate tested `algo_version NOT LIKE '1.7.%'`, a staleness proxy from when 1.7.x was the
   only writer; there are now eight and seven do not match, so this step and the thin-sales guard were
   overwriting each other every tick. **99.6% of admissions were false.**
3. **`/insights/pack-reality` was telling the public "No +EV packs right now"** while `pack_ev_latest`
   held 4,640 fresh rows. The 48 h freshness clause alone emptied the board; the three survivors were
   107–130 h stale behind the dead Top Shot endpoint.
4. **`sales-counterparty-backfill` has walked past Flow's spork wall** and burns ~9.2 h/day for zero —
   because Flow REST answers pruned history with **HTTP 200 + `execution:"Pending"` + zero events**, so
   an unreachable era is indistinguishable from a throttle miss. Filed; the fix needs `wrangler`.

**Corrections I made to my own work, in order**
- Bracketed a constant `171` with the fallback finding because the log lines were adjacent. All 171 have
  zero paid sales, so that step could never reach them. Two numbers on adjacent lines are not a
  relationship.
- Sized that backlog at "4,277 convertible" using the very predicate that was defining it wrongly. The
  real figure was ~13–31. **Circular, and the more useful lesson than the SQL.**
- Claimed the redundant writes would "disappear from `fmv_snapshots`" — unmeasurable that way, because
  the table is delete-then-insert and row counts by hour measure survivors, not writes.
- Read `[exited with code 0]` from a piped `npm test` over a run whose own summary said two tests failed.
- Queried Vercel with a `since` timestamp in the **future**, got zero deployments, and nearly read it as
  "no deploy fired".
- Hit **instance seven** of the unanchored conflict-marker false positive, an hour after reading the
  paragraph that documents it. That is what prompted turning the recipe into a script.

**Refuted before acting**
- The queued `edition_fmv_current` swap would have **corrected 41 editions' confidence wrongly and
  corrupted 4** — membership agreed perfectly (27,170 = 27,170) while the column being filtered on did
  not. Third independent time that table has been measured non-substitutable.
- A filed fix for `offers-sweep`'s 530 alternation would have **disarmed the upstream breaker**. The
  alternation is arithmetic: 30 min window ÷ 20 min tick = one real attempt per two ticks, a period-2
  square wave with no jitter. The `ok=false` is load-bearing state.
- The `paginateGroup` chunking #47 names as its durable fix is **not indicated** by the evidence.

**Guards that caught me, and were right**
- `insights-client-dates-are-hydration-safe-guard` reddened on a `Date.now()`. My reasoning was sound but
  the justification sat in a block comment above the `useMemo`; the guard requires it **inline within 4
  lines of the call**, because an escape placed anywhere in the file is an allowlist with extra steps.
- The inbox INDEX count guard caught a stale header twice, both times from concurrent sessions.
